), and the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\eta R_{K}

\displaystyle\leq M+\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(z_{K}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}\left(z_{K}\right)\right|+\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(z_{K}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(z_{0}\right)\right|+\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(z_{0}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}\left(z_{0}\right)\right|.

(36)

We assume that the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
converges everywhere on
\mathcal{Z}
. Thus, the third term in (
36
) vanishes since
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is conserved under the iterations of (
5
), and hence likewise by DAMD (
11
) and then AMD (
9
). Furthermore, by assuming that
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}(z_{k})-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}(z_{k})
is bounded by
G>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we bound (
36
) from above as follows:

\displaystyle\eta R_{K}\leq M+G+0+G=M+2G\qquad\iff\qquad R_{K}\leq\frac{1}{\eta}\left(M+2G\right).

The rest of the proof follows analogously to the proof for Theorem
4
as it does after (
34
).
∎

Hence, when the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
converges globally and its second-order correction
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}
remains uniformly bounded along the iterates, Theorem
5
implies that AMD attains the same algorithmic complexity as proximal (implicit) mirror descent
[
56
,
85
]
.

As an application of Theorem
5
, Theorem
3
identifies the quadratic setting as a case where absolute convergence of
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
can be established rigorously. This setting is equivalent to Alternating Gradient Descent (AGD) as studied by
[
8
]
, whose Theorem 1 proves bounded regret by a different argument.

In this case, we have
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
F(p)=p^{\top}Bp
and
G(q)=q^{\top}Cq
with
B
and
C
positive-definite, and regularizers with conjugates

\displaystyle\alpha(a)=\frac{1}{4}a^{\top}B^{-1}a\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\alpha^{*}(p)=p^{\top}Bp,\qquad\beta(b)=\frac{1}{4}b^{\top}C^{-1}b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta^{*}(q)=q^{\top}Cq,

such that
F=\alpha^{*}
and
G=\beta^{*}
. The modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
converges absolutely whenever
\eta^{2}\sigma_{\max}(BC)<1
. Furthermore, when
\eta^{2}\sigma_{\max}(B)\sigma_{\max}(C)<1
, Theorem 5 in
[
8
]
implies that the symplectic Euler (or equivalently, AGD) iterates lie on compact quadratic level sets (ellipsoids). The corresponding Bregman divergences are the weighted Euclidean (Mahalanobis) distances

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(a_{k},a_{0})=\frac{1}{4}\|a_{k}-a_{0}\|_{B^{-1}}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(a_{k}-a_{0})^{\top}B^{-1}(a_{k}-a_{0}),

\displaystyle D_{\beta}(b_{k},b_{0})=\frac{1}{4}\|b_{k}-b_{0}\|_{C^{-1}}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(b_{k}-b_{0})^{\top}C^{-1}(b_{k}-b_{0}),

and hence, these remain uniformly bounded for all iterates
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, since
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
converges absolutely on these compact ellipsoids by Theorem
3
, the remainder
\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\eta^{j}H_{j}(z_{k})
is uniformly bounded for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, by applying Theorem
5
, we recover the
\mathcal{O}(K^{-1})
time-average regret obtained in
[
8
]
.

However, to show that the conditions from Theorem
5
hold in the quadratic case, we used the fact that the symplectic Euler iterates lie on compact ellipsoids. As we noted at the end of Section
7
, bounded iterates already imply a bounded regret by Lemma
4
:

Corollary 2
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be closed and convex, and let

z_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}
denote the symplectic Euler iterates with stepsize
\eta>0
.
Assume the following:

1.

For some
C>0
, the dual iterates
z_{k}=\left(p_{k},q_{k}\right)
satisfy
\left|\left|p_{k}-p_{0}\right|\right|,\left|\left|q_{k}-q_{0}\right|\right|\leq C
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and

2.

\nabla F=\nabla\alpha^{*}\left(U(\cdot)\right)
and
\nabla G=\nabla\beta^{*}\left(V(\cdot)\right)
are continuous on
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, respectively.

Define the closed balls
\mathbb{B}_{p}:=\{p\in\mathcal{P}:\left|\left|p-p_{0}\right|\right|\leq C\}
and
\mathbb{B}_{q}:=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}:\left|\left|q-q_{0}\right|\right|\leq C\}
. By continuity, the following suprema are finite:

L_{F}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{B}_{p}}{\left|\left|\nabla F(u)\right|\right|},\quad L_{G}:=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}_{q}}{\left|\left|\nabla G(v)\right|\right|},\quad M_{F}:=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{B}_{p}}{\left|F(u)\right|},\quad M_{G}:=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}_{q}}{\left|G(v)\right|}.

Let
L:=\max\{L_{F},L_{G}\}
and
M:=\max\{M_{F},M_{G}\}
. Then, for every horizon
K\in\mathbb{N}
,

R_{K}\leq\frac{4}{\eta}\left(M+CL\right)+L^{2}.

In particular,
R_{K}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\overline{\mathrm{dg}}_{K}=\mathcal{O}(K^{-1})
.

Proof.

We start from (
24
) in Lemma
4
with
H=F+G
and
z_{k}=\left(p_{k},q_{k}\right)
:

\displaystyle R_{K}\leq\frac{1}{\eta}\left(D_{H}\left(z_{0},z_{K}\right)+\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}\left(z_{K}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(1)}\left(z_{0}\right)\right).

(37)

Note that
D_{H}\left(z_{0},z_{K}\right)=D_{F}\left(p_{0},p_{K}\right)+D_{G}\left(q_{0},q_{K}\right)
. Moreover, by convexity,

\displaystyle 0\leq D_{F}(p_{0},p_{K})

\displaystyle=F(p_{0})-F(p_{K})-\left<\nabla F(p_{K}),p_{0}-p_{K}\right>

\displaystyle\leq|F(p_{0})|+|F(p_{K})|+\left|\left|\nabla F(p_{K})\right|\right|\left|\left|p_{K}-p_{0}\right|\right|.

(38)

By assumption,
\left|F(p_{0})\right|,\left|F(p_{K})\right|\leq M
,
\left|\left|\nabla F(p_{K})\right|\right|\leq L
, and
\left|\left|p_{K}-p_{0}\right|\right|\leq C
. Hence, we can bound (
38
) as follows:

\displaystyle D_{F}(p_{0},p_{K})\leq M+M+LC=2M+LC.

The same bound on
D_{G}\left(q_{0},q_{K}\right)
follows analogously, whence we have

\displaystyle D_{H}\left(z_{0},z_{K}\right)\leq 2\left(2M+CL\right).

(39)

Next, recall that

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{(1)}_{\eta}(p,q)=F(p)+G(q)-\frac{\eta}{2}\left<\nabla F(p),\nabla G(q)\right>.

Thus,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}^{(1)}_{\eta}(z_{K})-\widetilde{H}^{(1)}_{\eta}(z_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\leq|F(p_{K})-F(p_{0})|+|G(q_{K})-G(q_{0})|

(40a)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta}{2}\left|\left<\nabla F(p_{K}),\nabla G(q_{K})\right>-\left<\nabla F(p_{0}),\nabla G(q_{0})\right>\right|.

(40b)

By the mean value inequality,

\displaystyle|F(p_{K})-F(p_{0})|\leq L\left|\left|p_{K}-p_{0}\right|\right|\leq LC\quad\textrm{and}\quad|G(q_{K})-G(q_{0})|\leq LC,

(41)

and by applying the triangle and Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities to (
40
),

\displaystyle\left|\left<\nabla F(p_{K}),\nabla G(q_{K})\right>-\left<\nabla F(p_{0}),\nabla G(q_{0})\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left<\nabla F(p_{K}),\nabla G(q_{K})\right>\right|+\left|\left<\nabla F(p_{0}),\nabla G(q_{0})\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\nabla F(p_{K})\right|\right|\left|\left|\nabla G(q_{K})\right|\right|+\left|\left|\nabla F(p_{0})\right|\right|\left|\left|\nabla G(q_{0})\right|\right|

\displaystyle\leq(L)(L)+(L)(L)

\displaystyle=2L^{2}.

(42)

Hence, using (
41
) and (
42
) to bound (
40
) from above, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}^{(1)}_{\eta}(z_{K})-\widetilde{H}^{(1)}_{\eta}(z_{0})\right|\leq LC+LC+\frac{\eta}{2}\left(2L^{2}\right)=2LC+\eta L^{2}.

(43)

Finally, using (
39
) and (
43
) together to bound (
37
) from above,

\displaystyle\boxed{R_{K}\leq\frac{4}{\eta}\left(M+CL\right)+L^{2}.}

We then proceed as we did in the proof for Theorem
4
after (
34
).
∎

Corollary
2
shows that when the symplectic Euler iterates remain bounded, the total regret is also bounded under mild assumptions, thereby providing a purely geometric route to proving bounded regret that does not rely on the existence or convergence of the modified Hamiltonian.

Consequently, in the quadratic case, Corollary
2
renders Theorem
5
unnecessary: The presence of bounded iterates already implies bounded regret, even though this case is among the few for which
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is known to converge absolutely. As mentioned already in Section
6
, for other cases, rigorous proofs of convergence for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
remain scarce, and counterexamples are well documented
[
73
,
31
,
4
]
. Therefore, identifying general, practically verifiable conditions under which a Hamiltonian
H
yields a convergent modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is an important open problem—one whose resolution would make Theorem
5
an analytic foundation for proving bounded regret in AMD.

Finally, we note that a bounded regret might seem superficially obvious from the start, since if the original Hamiltonian is closed, proper, and coercive convex (e.g., for an ellipsoid),
then its level sets are convex and bounded

[
67
, Section 8]
. However, that is for the Hamiltonian flow generated by the
unperturbed
Hamiltonian
H
. The implication here is that even the Hamiltonian flow generated by the
modified
Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is associated with a bounded regret in discrete time, albeit under assumptions on
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
.

10
Conclusion

With regards to energy conservation, there are symplectic integrators which tend to conserve the Hamiltonian better than symplectic Euler for the same stepsize—the leapfrog and implicit midpoint integrators are some examples; see, e.g.,
[
17
]
and Chapter I in
[
35
]
for further details and numerical illustrations. There are also algorithms with better performance than AMD for the setting introduced in Section
4
, such as simultaneous proximal mirror descent
[
56
,
85
]
. However, we are mainly interested in studying symplectic Euler because, when the original Hamiltonian is convex, symplectic Euler is the dual space representation of AMD. Moreover, we are interested in studying AMD because it serves as a relatively simple proof of concept for breaching the barrier between the fields of symplectic integration and theoretical computer science. We encourage future studies which use such connections, e.g., by using known symplectic integrators which tend to perform well and seeing if these can be translated to an analogous algorithm in a game-theoretic setting.

Since the field of symplectic integration (let alone Hamiltonian dynamics as a whole) has existed for over three decades, there is no doubt that solid results and rigorous error bounds already exist regarding the trajectories and energy conservation of said methods. And for countless applications in computational physics, chemistry, and biology, these have proved to be sufficient
[
28
,
71
,
20
, e.g.,]
. However, in the setting of Hamiltonian dynamics as it appears in sampling, optimization, and games, we are more interested in the algorithmic implications and guarantees which such methods might provide. We hope that this paper not only provides a foundation of properties and concepts—a framework—that demonstrates how new and old results in symplectic numerical analysis can be used in such contexts, but also reveals how many more questions remain unanswered.

Appendix A
Lie algebra and Poisson brackets

Lie algebra.

Recall a
Lie algebra

L
is a vector space (over
\mathbb{R}
in our case) with a
Lie bracket
operation
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]\colon L\times L\to L
which is bilinear, anticommutative (
[\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]=-[\ell_{2},\ell_{1}]
for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in L
), and satisfies the Jacobi identity (
[\ell_{1},[\ell_{2},\ell_{3}]]+[\ell_{2},[\ell_{3},\ell_{1}]]+[\ell_{3},[\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]]=0
for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\in L
).
For further references on Lie algebra, see e.g.
[
36
,
40
,
81
]
.

Adjoint representation.

Recall that for any Lie algebra
L
equipped with Lie bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]
, for any element
\ell\in L
we can define the
adjoint representation
of
\ell
:

\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq\left[\ell,\cdot\right]

as a linear function on
L
.

We define the
adjoint action
of an element
\ell\in L
by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq\left[\ell,\cdot\right].

(44)

This means for any
\widetilde{\ell}\in L
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}\left(\widetilde{\ell}\right)=\left[\ell,\widetilde{\ell}\right].

(45)

We also define the
k
-fold application of adjoint actions
\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}^{k}
by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}^{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}

(46)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}^{k}(\mathrm{ad}_{\ell}(\cdot)).

(47)

With the Poisson bracket as the Lie bracket, this is equivalent to iterated Poisson bracket:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}^{k}=\{\{\{...\{\cdot,\underbrace{\varphi\},\varphi\},\varphi}_{k~\text{times}}\}=\{\cdot,\varphi^{k}\},

(48)

where
\varphi\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
.

Poisson brackets.

For
F,G\in C^{n}(\mathcal{Z})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we recall their
Poisson bracket

\{F,G\}\in C^{n-1}(\mathcal{Z})
is the function given by:

\displaystyle\{F,G\}=-(\nabla_{p}F)^{\top}(\nabla_{q}G)+(\nabla_{q}F)^{\top}(\nabla_{p}G).

(49)

More precisely, for any
(p,q)\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\displaystyle\{F,G\}(p,q)=-(\nabla_{p}F(p,q))^{\top}(\nabla_{q}G(p,q))+(\nabla_{q}F(p,q))^{\top}(\nabla_{p}G(p,q)).

Note that we can also write the Poisson bracket as an inner product of the gradient vectors of
F
and
G
with respect to the symplectic matrix
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}
:

\displaystyle\{F,G\}(p,q)=(\nabla F(p,q))^{\top}\,\Omega\,\nabla G(p,q).

Iterated Poisson brackets.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
r_{1},\dots,r_{N},s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the
iterated Poisson brackets (IPBs)
of
F
and
G
as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{1}}\cdots F^{s_{N}}G^{r_{N}}\right\}=\{\cdots\underbrace{\left\{\left\{F,\dots,F\right\},F\right\}}_{s_{1}},\underbrace{\left.\left.\left.G\right\},\cdots G\right\},G\right\}}_{r_{1}},\cdots\underbrace{\left.\left.\left.F\right\},\cdots F\right\},F\right\}}_{s_{N}},\underbrace{\left.\left.\left.\left.G\right\},\cdots\right\}G\right\},G\right\}}_{r_{N}}.

Here we assume
F
and
G
are differentiable as many times as necessary for the definition above to make sense.
The definition of Poisson brackets (
49
) corresponds to the case
N=1
,
r_{1}=s_{1}=1
:
\{F^{1}G^{1}\}=\{F,G\}
, and we can define the general IPB inductively.
Using this notation, we can write for example
\{\{F,G\},G\}=\{F^{1}G^{2}\}
, and

\{FG^{4}F^{2}\}=\{\{\{\{\{\{F,G\},G\},G\},G\},F\},F\}.

One can check that the Poisson bracket satisfies the axioms for a Lie bracket. Thus, results for Lie algebra also apply to those for a space of
C^{\infty}
functions equipped with a Poisson bracket.

Free Lie algebra.

Let
F,G\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z})
.
Consider the subspace
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
of
C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
of functions generated by the linear combinations of
F
,
G
, and their IPBs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)\coloneqq\text{Span}\left\{\left\{F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{1}}\cdots F^{s_{N}}G^{r_{N}}\right\}\colon r_{1},s_{1},\dots,r_{N},s_{N}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},N\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

(50)

The vector space
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
equipped with the Poisson bracket
\left\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\}
defines a Lie algebra; this is called the
free Lie algebra
generated by
F
and
G
. For a more general definition of free Lie algebra and some properties, see Chapter 3 from
[
81
]
.

Exponential map.

For some
\eta>0
, we can formally expand the exponential
\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}\right)
of the adjoint representation
\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}
of some
\varphi\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
via the repeated application of
\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}
, which can be expressed as IPBs:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}^{k}}{k!}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}\{\{\{...\{\cdot,\overbrace{\varphi\},\varphi\},\varphi\},...,\varphi}^{k}\}}{k!}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}\{\cdot,\varphi^{k}\}}{k!}}

(51)

See
[
81
, Chapter 2]
or
[
36
, Chapters 3-5]
for how the exponential of an adjoint representation (
51
) generalizes over other Lie algebras.

Hamiltonian flow from Poisson bracket.

The time evolution of the phase space variables
z=\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
under a Hamiltonian flow (
1
) is generated by the Poisson bracket operation with
H
(see, e.g., Section 5.2 in
[
41
]
or Section 1.3 in
[
5
]
):

\displaystyle\dot{z}(t)=\left\{z,H(z)\right\}=\Omega\nabla H\left(z(t)\right).

(52)

Then we can integrate (
52
) from
t=0
to
t=\eta
to get

\displaystyle z(\eta)=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{H}\right)z(0).

(53)

Appendix B
The modified Hamiltonian and the BCH series

We now discuss how to construct the modified Hamiltonian for the symplectic Euler method via the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series.
To explain the results, we use the tools from Lie algebra and representation theory introduced in Appendix
A
.

B.1
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series

For any
x,y\in L
, the value of
z
which solves
\exp\left(x\right)\exp\left(y\right)=\exp\left(z\right)
can be given via the Dynkin form of the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula

[
40
]
in
Dynkin form
, which we call the
BCHD series
:

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\log\left(\exp\left(x\right)\exp\left(y\right)\right)

(54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left[x^{r_{1}}y^{s_{1}}x^{r_{2}}y^{s_{2}}\cdots x^{r_{n}}y^{s_{n}}\right]}{\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

(55)

Here for
r_{1},s_{1},\dots,r_{n},s_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

\displaystyle\left[x^{r_{1}}y^{s_{1}}x^{r_{2}}y^{s_{2}}\cdots x^{r_{n}}y^{s_{n}}\right]=[\underbrace{x,[x,\dotsm[x}_{r_{1}},[\underbrace{y,[y,\dotsm[y}_{s_{1}},\,\dotsm\,[\underbrace{x,[x,\dotsm[x}_{r_{n}},[\underbrace{y,[y,\dotsm y}_{s_{n}}]]\dotsm]]].

See also
[
3
]
and references therein for an overview of different proofs of the BCH formula for the Dynkin form and others.

In particular, when
L=\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
and the Poisson bracket is the Lie bracket, we can define the adjoint representation
[
36
]

\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq\left\{\cdot,\varphi\right\}
of
any
function
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
—i.e., the linear map defined by the Poisson bracket of a function in
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
with
\varphi
. Furthermore, instantiating (
55
) with
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
and the Poisson bracket gives the same expression as (
55
), except with the iterated Lie brackets as IPBs
in reverse order
.
1
1

1

This is because when defining the adjoint representation over a general Lie algebra
L
vs.
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
, we swap the ordering of the arguments for a general Lie bracket vs. the Poisson bracket, respectively. The derivation of the BCH formula involves interpreting
\exp\left(x\right)
and
\exp\left(y\right)
via their adjoint representations, thereby leading to an expansion in terms of iterated Lie brackets.

B.2
Deriving the modified Hamiltonian

In summary, the Dynkin form (
20
) of the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is derived as a formal expansion by constructively working from the initial assumptions that there is a Hamiltonian flow (
2
) generated by some modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
which interpolates the iterates of, and is thereby conserved by, the iterates of the symplectic Euler method (
5
). Hence, we first show that the iterations of the symplectic Euler method (
5
) conserve
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
under the convergence conditions outlined in Theorem
2
. But at first, we provide a proof sketch of Theorem
2
, following the expositions in
[
31
,
4
]
.

Proof sketch.

Recall from (
53
) that Hamiltonian flow (
2
) is equivalent to exponentiating the adjoint operation of the Hamiltonian function:

\displaystyle z(\eta)=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{H}\right)z(0).

Furthermore, we can expand

\displaystyle\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{H}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{H}^{k}}{k!}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}\{\cdot,H^{k}\}}{k!}}.

(56)

Analogously (see Appendix
B.2
), the flow generated by the symplectic Euler method (
5
) can be expressed as iterations of
\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)
acting on
z(0)
.
Then we want to find a modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
for the flow generated by
\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)
.
That is, we want to find
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
such that

\displaystyle\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right),

(57)

or equivalently, such that

\displaystyle\left(p_{k+1},q_{k+1}\right)=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)\left(p_{k},q_{k}\right),

since
z(t=0)=\left(p_{k},q_{k}\right)
and
z\left(t=\eta\right)=\left(p_{k+1},q_{k+1}\right)
. Taking the formal inverse of the exponential operation in (
57
) gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}=\frac{1}{\eta}\log\left(\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\right).

(58)

We expand (
58
) formally via the BCHD series
20
in terms of Lie brackets on the set of automorphisms
\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)\right)
of
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}=\mathrm{ad}_{F}+\mathrm{ad}_{G}+\frac{\eta}{2}\left[\mathrm{ad}_{F},\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right]+\frac{\eta^{2}}{12}\left(\left[\mathrm{ad}_{F},\left[\mathrm{ad}_{F},\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right]\right]+\left[\mathrm{ad}_{G},\left[\mathrm{ad}_{G},\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right]\right]\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\eta^{3}\right),

(59)

where
\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]=\Phi\Psi-\Psi\Phi
for any
\Phi,\Psi\in\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)\right)
.
The Jacobi identity and the anti-commutativity of the Poisson bracket (e.g., Section 1.3 in
[
5
]
or Section 5.2 in
[
41
]
) imply that

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\left\{\psi,\varphi\right\}}=\left[\mathrm{ad}_{\varphi},\mathrm{ad}_{\psi}\right]

(60)

for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
.
Combining (
59
) and (
60
), we derive the adjoint representation of (
20
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\mathrm{ad}_{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}}}{\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

Hence, tracing our steps backwards from here after using the series (
20
) as an ansatz for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
, we find the formula for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
.

We note there are simpler forms of the BCH series, such as the celebrated Goldberg formula which expresses the order-by-order form of the BCH formula as a sum of (generally, non-commutative) products of
F
and
G

in the Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
2
2

2

The product
\oslash
of any two
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
is defined as
\varphi\oslash\psi\coloneqq\nabla_{q}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{p}\psi
.
scaled by the so-called Goldberg coefficients
[
58
]
or a similar form which writes the terms via Lie brackets (or in particular, Poisson brackets) of
F
and
G

[
77
]
. These Goldberg formulae are useful for computations in practice and for simplifying the number of terms in the sum, but we will not use them in this paper.

Furthermore, there is also a recursive formula for the BCH series
[
20
,
81
]
which expresses each successive order-by-order correction
H_{j}
to the BCH series in terms of a sum of iterated Lie brackets (or IPBs when working with Poisson brackets in particular), the latter of which are taken over prior terms in the BCH series. Lemma 2.15.3 in
[
81
]
gives the most general form of this recursive formula over a general Lie algebra, but for this study, (
198
) and (
199
) give the particular formula when applied to computing the order-by-order corrections
H_{j}
of
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
. We implement this formula for
H_{j}
in a computer program to aid us in proving Lemma
19
and Conjecture
1
up to
N=10
for
d=1
—see Appendix
F.2
for more details.

Since determining the convergence of the
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
series (
20
) is needed to use Theorem
2
, this will be mentioned in Section
6
. However, even when such convergence cannot be assumed, (
20
) still proves to be useful as a formal expansion, as we will show in Section
7
.

Connecting symplectic Euler with the Poisson bracket.

There is a formal connection between translation by the gradient of a function, as seen in each of the two steps of symplectic Euler method (
5
), and multiplication by the exponential of the adjoint representation (in the sense of Lie algebra representation) of that function. This connection can be summarized via the following lemma:

Lemma 5
.

Let
F:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
G:\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be differentiable over
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively. For all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{Z}
,
3
3

3

We remind the reader that
\mathrm{ad}_{F}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq\left\{\cdot,F\right\}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{G}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq\left\{\cdot,G\right\}
, as defined in Section
A
.

\displaystyle\left.\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\right|_{q=q}p=p-\eta\nabla_{q}G\left(q\right),

(61)

and for all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\displaystyle\left.\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\right|_{p=p}q=q+\eta\nabla_{p}F\left(p\right).

(62)

Proof.

We set out to prove (
61
); the proof for (
62
) follows the same line of reasoning analogously. Let
p\in\mathcal{P}
. We introducing the time-like variable
0\leq s\leq\eta
and define the trivial ODE

\displaystyle\frac{dx\left(s\right)}{ds}=\left.\mathrm{ad}_{G}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{p}=x(s)}=-\nabla_{q}G\left(q\right),

(63)

and let
x\left(0\right)=p
.
4
4

4

For notational ease, we assume that both sides of (
63
) are evaluated at
q
and the Poisson bracket derivatives are taken with respect to
\widetilde{p}
and
q
rather than
p
and
q
, respectively.
We can solve for
x(s)
evaluated at
s=\eta
by directly integrating (
63
) as follows:

\displaystyle x\left(\eta\right)

\displaystyle=x\left(0\right)-\int_{0}^{\eta}{\nabla_{q}G\left(q\right)ds}

\displaystyle=p-\eta\nabla_{q}G\left(q\right).

(64)

Hence,
x\left(\eta\right)
is equal to the right-hand side of (
61
). Furthermore, we can take the Taylor series of
x\left(\eta\right)
about
\eta=0
as

\displaystyle x\left(\eta\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}}{k!}\left.\frac{d^{k}x\left(s\right)}{ds^{k}}\right|_{s=0}}

(65)

for all
\left|\eta\right|
sufficiently small. (Bear for a moment as to how small.) To rewrite
\frac{d^{k}x\left(s\right)}{ds^{k}}
in terms of
\mathrm{ad}_{G}
and
p
, note that by (
63
),

\displaystyle\frac{dx}{ds}=\left.\mathrm{ad}_{G}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{p}=x(s)}.

(66)

From here, we claim that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{k}x}{ds^{k}}=\left.\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{p}=x(s)}

(67)

for all
k\geq 0
. The case for
k=0
is trivially true and (
66
) establishes
k=1
. However, for any
k\geq 2
, since
\left\{p,G\right\}=-\nabla_{q}G
and
\left\{q,F\right\}=\nabla_{p}F
, we have
\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k}p=\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k-2}\left\{\left\{p,G\right\},G\right\}=\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k-2}\left\{-\nabla_{q}G,G\right\}=\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k-2}0=0
and
\mathrm{ad}_{F}^{k}q=\mathrm{ad}_{F}^{k-2}\left\{\left\{q,F\right\},F\right\}=\mathrm{ad}_{F}^{k-2}\left\{\nabla_{p}F,F\right\}=\mathrm{ad}_{F}^{k-2}0=0
. But this should be expected by (
63
), since for
k\geq 2
,

\frac{d^{k}x}{ds^{k}}=\frac{d^{k-1}}{ds^{k-1}}\frac{dx}{ds}=\frac{d^{k-1}}{ds^{k-1}}\left[-\nabla_{q}G\left(q\right)\right]=0

likewise. Therefore, using (
67
), (
65
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle x\left(\eta\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}}{k!}\left.\frac{d^{k}x\left(s\right)}{ds^{k}}\right|_{s=0}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k}}{k!}\left.\mathrm{ad}_{G}^{k}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{p}=x\left(0\right)}}=\left.\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\right|_{q=q}x(0)=\left.\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\right|_{q=q}p.

(68)

By (
64
) and (
68
), we conclude.
∎

We note that Lemma
5
is, in a sense, trivial, since the terms in the series expansions for the exponentials in (
61
) and (
62
) vanish after two terms. After all, the two component flows under
G
(applied to
p
) and then
F
(applied to
q
) each involve a mere translation when considered separately. However, our goal here is to find an equivalent
Hamiltonian
flow for the composition of these two flows at once—i.e., the flow induced by the modified Hamiltonian,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
, in the sense of (
2
)—which turns out to be highly nontrivial. We introduce the exponential map notation because the algebraic properties of the exponential map will be used to derive
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
and show that it is conserved under the symplectic Euler method.

Conservation of the modified Hamiltonian under symplectic Euler.

Here we state our proof of Theorem
2
.

Proof.

Making necessary assumptions on
F
and
G
to use Lemma
5
, we rewrite the next iteration after applying symplectic Euler to some initial point
\left(p,q\right)
as

\displaystyle\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\left(p,q\right)

(69)

using the following notation:

(1)

\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)
is applied to the first argument with
\mathrm{ad}_{G}
evaluated at the second argument;

(2)

\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)
returns the results after application (as described in (1)) in the first argument and leaves the second argument unchanged; and

(3)

\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)
does the same as in (1) and (2) except with the two arguments switched.

Hence, by (
69
), to find a new modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
whose associated continuous-time Hamiltonian system interpolates the discrete-time trajectories of the symplectic Euler method (
5
), we want
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
to satisfy

\displaystyle\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\left(p,q\right)=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)\left(p,q\right)

(70)

for all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{Z}
, where
\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)
is evaluated component-wise to
\left(p,q\right)
.

As covered in
[
36
, Chapter 3]
, (
70
) can be rewritten using the adjoint map
\mathrm{ad}:\mathbb{L}\left(F,G\right)\rightarrow\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)\right)
:
5
5

5

In the context of abstract algebra, an automorphism is a bijective homomorphism of an algebraic structure (e.g., a group, ring, or Lie algebra) with itself
[
40
,
36
]
. For any algebraic structure
\mathcal{A}
,
\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
is the set of automorphisms on
\mathcal{A}
;
\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
is itself a group.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &&\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{F}\right)\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{G}\right)\left(p,q\right)&=\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)\left(p,q\right)\\
&\iff&\exp\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\eta F}\right)\exp\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\eta G}\right)\left(p,q\right)&=\exp\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\eta\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)\left(p,q\right)\\
&\iff&\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta F\right)}\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta G\right)}\left(p,q\right)&=\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\right)}\left(p,q\right),\end{aligned}

where
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
is the free Lie algebra generated by
F
and
G
and
\mathbb{L}\left(F,G\right)
is the Lie group generated by
F
and
G
under the group operation
f\oslash g=\nabla_{p}f\cdot\nabla_{q}g
, as defined in Section
A
. This follows due to the commuting diagram between
\mathbb{L}\left(F,G\right)
,
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
,
\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{L}\left(F,G\right)\right)
, and the Lie algebra of
\textrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{L}\left(F,G\right)\right)
(Theorem 3.28 and Proposition 3.35 from
[
36
]
), and how these apply to the adjoint map
\mathrm{ad}
. Furthermore, since
\mathrm{ad}
is a group homomorphism,
\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta F\right)}\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta G\right)}=\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta F\right)\exp\left(\eta G\right)}
, and hence the equations above is equivalent with

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta F\right)\exp\left(\eta G\right)}\left(p,q\right)=\mathrm{ad}_{\exp\left(\eta\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\right)}\left(p,q\right).

(71)

If we take
\eta\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
to be the Dynkin form of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series applied to
\eta F
and
\eta G
(see Section
A
), then it follows that

\displaystyle\eta\widetilde{H}_{\eta}=\log\left(\exp\left(\eta F\right)\exp\left(\eta G\right)\right),

from which we immediately deduce (
71
). Therefore, we have shown (
70
), provided that
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
as defined using the BCH series converges.

Finally, by (
70
), when the BCH series for
F
and
G
converges, applying one iteration of symplectic Euler to
\left(p,q\right)
is equivalent with solving the system of ODEs

\displaystyle\frac{dx}{ds}=\left.\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{p}=x}=-\nabla_{y}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x,y\right),\quad\frac{dy}{ds}=\left.\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\left(\widetilde{q}\right)\right|_{\widetilde{q}=y}=\nabla_{x}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x,y\right)

(72)

from
s=0
to
s=\eta
with initial condition
\left(x(0),y(0)\right)=\left(p,q\right)
(see, e.g., Sections III.4, IX.3, and IX.9 of
[
35
]
, and Section 6.3.2 of
[
41
]
). Thus, using (
72
), we confirm that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)=\nabla_{x}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)\cdot\frac{dx}{ds}+\nabla_{y}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)\cdot\frac{dy}{ds}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{x}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)\cdot\nabla_{y}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)+\nabla_{y}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(s),y(s)\right)=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(0),y(0)\right)=\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(x(\eta),y(\eta)\right)\Rightarrow\boxed{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)p,\exp\left(\eta\mathrm{ad}_{\widetilde{H}_{\eta}}\right)q\right).}

(73)

Hence, Theorem
2
follows.
∎

B.3
The integral form of the BCH series

For the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula on
any
normed Lie algebra
\left(L,\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|\right)
, we propose the following equivalent formula to the Dynkin representation (
55
) which involves the adjoint representation in a way similar to Theorem 5.5 in
[
54
]
and Theorem 5.3 in
[
36
]
. These aforementioned theorems involve derivations of similar integral formulas as Lemma
6
below, which are related but not exactly the same.

Lemma 6
.

For any
A,B\in L
sufficiently small in norm—i.e., such that
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
or
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
for all
t\in\left[0,1\right]
,

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{A}e^{B}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}{\log\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{-1}\left(A+e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B\right)dt}.

(74)

Moreover, the series expansion of (
74
) converges absolutely.

For the sake of completeness, we provide two unrelated proofs of Lemma
6
separately in the end of this section, one of which is original and follows directly from the BCHD series (
55
) and the other of which uses similar techniques as those used in the proofs of Theorem 5.5 in
[
54
]
and Theorem 5.3 in
[
36
]
. The former assumes
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
while the latter assumes
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
. But before going into these two proofs, from Lemma
6
, we have a few corollaries. The first one deals with how Lemma
6
applies to the modified Hamiltonian (see
6
for details) of the symplectic Euler method.

As the first proof in Appendix
B.3
demonstrates, the
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
series
20
directly implies Lemma (
6
) and vice-versa under absolute convergence, and either form could be more useful in computing
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
depending on the functions
F
and
G
in a particular case. Moreover, recall from Appendix
A
that the Poisson bracket (or the Poisson bracket rescaled by
\eta
) is itself a Lie bracket over the free Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
generated by
F,G\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Furthermore, equip
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
with some function norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|
which is continuous over the space. Then, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
, we define the operator norm of
\varphi
,
\left|\left|\varphi\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}
, as the smallest
L>0
satisfying
\left|\left|\left\{\varphi,\psi\right\}\right|\right|\leq L\left|\left|\psi\right|\right|
for all
\psi\in\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
.

Thus, instantiating Lemma
6
with the Poisson bracket rescaled by
\eta
, we have the following:

Corollary 3
.

The modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
for symplectic Euler applied to the Hamiltonian
H=F\left(p\right)+G\left(q\right)
has the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}{\log\left(e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}\right)\left(e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}-I\right)^{-1}\left(G+e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}F\right)dt}

(75)

which converges absolutely whenever
\left|\left|e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
or
\left|\left|e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
for all
t\in\left[0,\eta\right]
.

Even in situations where we can express the adjoint representations as matrices, computing the integrand for Lemma
6
or Corollary
3
could be computationally prohibitive in practice. Hence, another option is to express the integrand as a single series, which Lemma
7
demonstrates below:

Lemma 7
.

Given the same setting as Lemma
6
except assuming
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
, we have

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{A}e^{B}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(I-e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)^{j}}{j+1}\left(A+e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B\right)dt}}.

Proof.

From p. 161 of
[
54
]
, we have the following series expansion for
\\
\log\left(X\right)\left(X-I\right)^{-1}
about
X=I
:

\displaystyle\log\left(X\right)\left(X-I\right)^{-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(I-X\right)^{j}}{j+1}},

(76)

which converges whenever
\left|\left|X-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
. Then, use (
76
) to expand the integrand of (
74
) in Lemma
6
.

We can similarly use (
76
) to expand the integrand of (
75
).
∎

Finally, by instantiating the Lie bracket in Lemma
7
with the Poisson bracket multiplied by
\eta
, we derive Theorem
6
:

Theorem 6
.

For any
F\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{P}\right)
and
G\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)
such that
\left|\left|e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
for all
t\in\left[0,1\right]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(I-e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}\right)^{j}}{j+1}\left(G+e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}F\right)dt}}.

(77)

Moreover, the series expansion of (
77
) converges absolutely.

The integral form of the modified Hamiltonian (Theorem
6
) allows for direct calculation of a conserved quantity along the iterations of the algorithm in the quadratic case, as we will see in Appendix
C
.

An original proof of Lemma
6
.

Proof.

We do the computations in reverse order, noting that the steps are reversible after establishing absolute convergence since we did not reorder the series and only rewrote the terms separately using integrals. We start with the BCHD series
55
, carefully separate out the sum when
n=1
vs.
n\geq 2
, and rewrite the iterated Lie brackets in terms of their adjoint representations:

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{A}e^{B}\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r}B}{\left(r+1\right)!}}+A

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\cdots\\
r_{n-1}+s_{n-1}>0\\
r_{n}\geq 0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r_{1}}\mathrm{ad}_{B}^{s_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r_{n-1}}\mathrm{ad}_{B}^{s_{n-1}}\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r_{n}}B}{\left(r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n-1}+s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n-1}+r_{n}+1\right)\cdot r_{n}!\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\cdots\\
r_{n-1}+s_{n-1}>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r_{1}}\mathrm{ad}_{B}^{s_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{ad}_{A}^{r_{n-1}}\mathrm{ad}_{B}^{s_{n-1}}A}{\left(r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n-1}+s_{1}+\cdots+s_{n-1}+1\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}\right)

This further equals to:

\displaystyle\log(e^{A}e^{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0\end{subarray}}{\int_{0}^{1}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r}B}{r!}dt}}+A+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\cdots\\
r_{n-1}+s_{n-1}>0\\
r_{n}\geq 0\end{subarray}}{\int_{0}^{1}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{1}}}{r_{1}!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s_{1}}}{s_{1}!}\cdots\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{n-1}}}{r_{n-1}!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s_{n-1}}}{s_{n-1}!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{n}}}{r_{n}!}}dt}B\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\cdots\\
r_{n-1}+s_{n-1}>0\end{subarray}}{\int_{0}^{1}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{1}}}{r_{1}!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s_{1}}}{s_{1}!}\cdots\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{n-1}}}{r_{n-1}!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s_{n-1}}}{s_{n-1}!}}dtA}\right).

(78)

Under absolute convergence, we can then swap the integrals and summations in (
78
) and then rewrite the result using the exponential Taylor series
e^{X}=\sum_{r\geq 0}{X^{r}/r!}
as follows:

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{A}e^{B}\right)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}Bdt}+A

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r+s>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r}}{r!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s}}{s!}}\right)^{n-1}\sum_{r_{n}\geq 0}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r_{n}}}{r_{n}!}}dt}B\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\int_{0}^{1}{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r+s>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}\right)^{r}}{r!}\frac{\left(t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}\right)^{s}}{s!}}\right)^{n-1}dt}A\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B+A\right)dt}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}{\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{n-1}\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B+A\right)dt}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}}\int_{0}^{1}{\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{n-1}\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B+A\right)dt}.

(79)

Finally, under absolute convergence again and the Taylor series
\log\left(X+I\right)=\sum_{r\geq 1}{\left(-1\right)^{r+1}X^{r}/r}
(which converges absolutely whenever
\left|\left|X\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
), (
79
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{n-1}}\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B+A\right)dt}=

\displaystyle\boxed{\int_{0}^{1}{\log\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{-1}\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B+A\right)dt},}

(80)

as we wanted to show. Note finally that absolute convergence of the series expansion of (
80
) is ensured by the bound on the exponentials of the adjoints stated in Lemma
6
, thereby ensuring absolute convergence for the original BCH series.
∎

A classical proof of Lemma
6
.

Proof.

Define the auxiliary function
C\left(t\right)=\log\left(e^{tA}e^{tB}\right)
for
0\leq t\leq 1
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}e^{C\left(t\right)}=\frac{d}{dt}\left[e^{tA}e^{tB}\right]=Ae^{tA}e^{tB}+e^{tA}e^{tB}B=Ae^{C\left(t\right)}+e^{C\left(t\right)}B.

(81)

Furthermore, by the work of
[
78
]
and
[
36
, Theorem 5.4]
, since
C\left(t\right)
is smooth, the leftmost expression in (
81
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}e^{C\left(t\right)}=e^{C\left(t\right)}\left(I-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}\right)\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}^{-1}\frac{dC(t)}{dt}.

(82)

Hence, by both (
81
) and (
82
), and noting that the inverse of
e^{X}
is
e^{-X}
,

\displaystyle\left(I-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}\right)\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}^{-1}\frac{dC}{dt}=e^{-C\left(t\right)}\frac{d}{dt}e^{C\left(t\right)}=e^{-C\left(t\right)}\left[Ae^{C\left(t\right)}+e^{C\left(t\right)}B\right]

\displaystyle=e^{-C\left(t\right)}Ae^{C\left(t\right)}+B.

(83)

For sufficiently small
A
and
B
(i.e.,
A
and
B
such that
\left|\left|e^{\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}=\left|\left|e^{t\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}<1
),
6
6

6

Indeed, because
Ad
is a Lie group homomorphism
[
36
]
, by Proposition 3.35 from
[
36
]
, we have
e^{\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}=\textrm{Ad}_{e^{C\left(t\right)}}=\textrm{Ad}_{e^{tA}e^{tB}}=\textrm{Ad}_{e^{tA}}\textrm{Ad}_{e^{tB}}=e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}
.
we can invert
\left(I-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}\right)\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}^{-1}
, and hence, (
83
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{dC}{dt}=\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}\left(I-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}\right)^{-1}\left[e^{-C\left(t\right)}Ae^{C\left(t\right)}+B\right].

(84)

Then, following the same steps used to go from (5.16) to (5.18) in
[
36
]
, (
84
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{dC}{dt}=\log\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{-1}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\left[e^{-C\left(t\right)}Ae^{C\left(t\right)}+B\right].

(85)

Using the fact that
\mathrm{ad}_{B}B=0
,
e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}B=e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B
. Furthermore, using the identities from
[
36
, p. 117]
and
[
54
, Lemma 5.3]
,

\displaystyle e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}e^{-C\left(t\right)}Ae^{C\left(t\right)}=e^{\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}e^{-C\left(t\right)}Ae^{C\left(t\right)}

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}e^{\mathrm{ad}_{-C\left(t\right)}}A=e^{\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{C\left(t\right)}}A=A,

such that (
85
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{dC}{dt}=\log\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{-1}\left(A+e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B\right).

(86)

Integrating (
86
) from
0
to
1
gives us

\displaystyle C\left(1\right)-C\left(0\right)=\int_{0}^{1}{\log\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}\right)\left(e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{B}}-I\right)^{-1}\left(A+e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B\right)dt}.

Since
C\left(1\right)=\log\left(e^{A}e^{B}\right)
and
C\left(0\right)=\log\left(e^{0}e^{0}\right)=\log\left(I\right)=0
, we are done.
∎

Appendix C
The modified Hamiltonian in the quadratic case

As a test case to demonstrate the potency of the integral forms of the MH (Corollary
3
and Theorem
6
), we now study the particular, fundamental case when
F=ap^{2},G=bq^{2}
, and
d=1
—the quadratic case. In this case, the function

\mathsf{S}\left(p,q\right)=ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2\eta abpq

is a closed-form conserved quantity throughout the iterations of the symplectic Euler method (
5
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}\left(p_{n},q_{n}\right)=\mathsf{S}\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)\quad\forall n

for any stepsize
\eta>0
. We encourage readers to verify this fact for themselves or see
[
8
,
85
]
for further details, which demonstrates how the above results also generalize for
d>1
. In theory, the function
\mathsf{S}\left(p,q\right)
should also appear in the modified Hamiltonian. And this is indeed the case: By applying symbolic calculation programs to calculate the first terms in the modified Hamiltonian, expressed as the series (
20
) when
f=ap^{2}
,
g=bq^{2}
, we reveal the following pattern in the first few terms of the series:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}(p,q)=\mathsf{S}\left(p,q\right)\left(1+\frac{2ab\eta^{2}}{3}+\frac{8(ab)^{2}\eta^{4}}{15}+\frac{16(ab)^{3}\eta^{6}}{35}+\frac{128(ab)^{4}\eta^{8}}{215}+\cdots\right).

(87)

This pattern encourages us to calculate the limit of the series using the integral form of the BCH formula (Theorem
6
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(I-e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}e^{t\left\{\cdot,F\right\}}\right)^{j}}{j+1}\left(G+e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}F\right)dt}},

directly. To the best of our knowledge, this has not been done in prior literature.

It is highly inefficient to compute the terms of the original series formula (
20
) for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
directly. The most efficient algorithm for symbolic calculation that the authors could implement takes several hours to obtain the first 13 terms. Thus, computing
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
via the integral formula could demonstrate its efficiency when compared to the traditional Dynkin series formula.

Furthermore, as (
87
) implies, the modified Hamiltonian in this case is the product of a conserved quantity as a function of
p,q
and a power series of
\eta
. For
\eta
large enough, the power series diverges, yet the conserved quantity still remains conserved along the iterations. Thus, this is a direct example demonstrating that a conserved quantity could persist even when the Dynkin series (
20
) diverges, which could perhaps be explained by analytic continuation. This could motivate further exploring the Dynkin series from the perspective of renormalization theory for more general functions (especially when no closed-form is known) to take care of cases when the Dynkin series diverges.

C.1
Functional space of the Iterated Poisson Brackets

We now apply Theorem
6
to compute the modified Hamiltonian in this case. We first have the following lemma regarding the structure of the Iterated Poisson Brackets (IPBs):

Lemma 8
.

F
,
G
, and any IPBs generated by
F
and
G
has the following quadratic form:

\{\{\{...\{F,G\},F\},G\},...,\}=Ap^{2}+Bpq+Cq^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}p^{2}&pq&q^{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A\\
B\\
C\end{bmatrix}

for some constants
A,B,C\in\mathbb{R}
dependent on the number of Poisson brackets and the permutation of the functions
F,G
in the IPB.

Proof.

This can be shown easily via induction. Since
\{G,F\}=-\{F,G\}=4adpq
, the base case holds. Suppose this is true for an IPB
I
. Then,
\{F,I\}=-\nabla_{p}F\frac{\partial I}{\partial q}=-(2ap)(2Cq+Bp)=-2aBp^{2}+4aCpq
. Similarly,
\{G,I\}=\nabla_{q}G\frac{\partial I}{\partial p}=(2dq)(2Ap+Bq)=2Bdq^{2}+4dApq
.
∎

We remark that it is not the usual case that IPBs lie in a finite-dimensional function space. In most cases, the function space will be infinite, as we will see in the next section, when we talk about the cases in which the convergence criteria fail to hold.

Thus, the functional space endowed by the Poisson bracket in this case is simply the space of pure second-order polynomials of
p
and
q
. In this case, there exists a matrix expression of the adjoint representations
\mathrm{ad}_{F}\coloneqq\left\{\cdot,F\right\}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{G}\coloneqq\left\{\cdot,G\right\}
on the free Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
generated by
F
and
G
and endowed with the Poisson bracket, as suggested by the following lemma:

Lemma 9
.

Given the previous representation, we have the matrix representations

\mathrm{ad}_{F}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&2a\\
0&0&0\\
0&4a&0\\
\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathrm{ad}_{G}=-\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&2b\\
4b&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}

when we choose the basis
\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}
for
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
.

Proof.

From Lemma
8
,
\varphi\in\textrm{Span}\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}\Rightarrow\left\{\varphi,F\right\},\left\{\varphi,G\right\}\in\textrm{Span}\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}
, but we desire to express this implication more explicitly. Let
\varphi\in\textrm{Span}\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}
, in which case
\varphi\left(p,q\right)=c_{2}p^{2}+c_{1}q^{2}+c_{0}pq
for some constants
c_{2},c_{1},c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, which can be represented in
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
as
\left(c_{2},c_{1},c_{0}\right)
when using the basis
\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}
. Routine computations show that

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi,p^{2}\right\}=2c_{0}p^{2}+4c_{1}pq,\quad\left\{\varphi,q^{2}\right\}=-2c_{0}q^{2}-4c_{2}pq,\quad\left\{\varphi,pq\right\}=2c_{1}q^{2}-2c_{2}p^{2}.

(88)

Since the Poisson bracket is linear in each component, (
88
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{F}\left(\varphi\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\varphi,F\right\}=\left\{\varphi,ap^{2}\right\}=\left(2a\right)c_{0}p^{2}+\left(4a\right)c_{1}pq,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{G}\left(\varphi\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\varphi,G\right\}=\left\{\varphi,dq^{2}\right\}=\left(-2d\right)c_{0}q^{2}+\left(-4d\right)c_{2}pq,

i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{F}\left(c_{2},c_{1},c_{0}\right)=\left(2ac_{0},0,4ac_{1}\right),\qquad\mathrm{ad}_{G}\left(c_{2},c_{1},c_{0}\right)=-\left(0,2dc_{0},4dc_{2}\right)

when expressed in the basis
\left\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\right\}
.∎

Note that the existence of matrix expressions for
\mathrm{ad}_{F}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{G}
should already be expected from Ado’s theorem
[
36
]
, which states that every finite-dimensional real Lie algebra (e.g.,
\mathcal{L}\left(F,G\right)
for
F
and
G
quadratic) is isomorphic to an algebra of matrices.

Since the adjoint matrix in this case is of finite dimension and nilpotent, we are motivated to compute the MH in this quadratic case by evaluating the formula proposed in Theorem
6
directly. Doing so gives us Theorem
3
and the observations that follow thereafter.

C.2
Proof of Theorem
3

C.2.1
Preliminary work

We start by manipulating Theorem
6
to make calculations a bit easier. Recall that in last section we derived the matrix representation for
\text{ad}_{G}
and
\text{ad}_{F}
, if we have access to the diagonalization of
e^{t\{\cdot,G\}}e^{t\{\cdot,F\}}=e^{t\text{ad}_{G}}e^{t\text{ad}_{F}}=PDP^{-1}
, we could dramatically simplify the integral form of the BCH series for the modified Hamiltonian (
77
) into the following form:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}{P\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}P^{-1}\left(G+e^{t\left\{\cdot,G\right\}}F\right)dt}.

(89)

Motivated by the aforementioned simplification, we start by calculating the matrix exponentials of the matrix expression of the adjoint representations and calculate its
diagonalization. With the help of SymPy
[
53
]
, we have the following:

\displaystyle e^{t\mathrm{ad}_{G}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
4b^{2}t^{2}&1&-2bt\\
-4bt&0&1\end{bmatrix},\qquad e^{t\mathrm{ad}_{F}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&4a^{2}t^{2}&2at\\
0&1&0\\
0&4at&1\end{bmatrix}.

(90)

We now calculate their product:

\displaystyle e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{G}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{F}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&8a^{2}t^{2}&2at\\
8b^{2}t^{2}&1+8abt^{2}+64a^{2}b^{2}t^{4}&2bt+16a^{2}bt^{3}\\
-4bt&4at-32a^{2}bt^{3}&1-8abt^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

(91)

With some help from SymPy
[
53
]
, we now calculate
P
and
D
in the diagonalization
e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{G}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{F}}=PDP^{-1}
:

\displaystyle P=\left[\begin{matrix}-\frac{1}{2bt}&\frac{-abt-i\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}{2b}&\frac{-abt+i\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}{2b}\\
-\frac{1}{2at}&\frac{-abt+i\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}{2a}&\frac{-abt-i\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}{2a}\\
1&1&1\end{matrix}\right].

(92)

Here
i=\sqrt{-1}
is the imaginary unit.

We can thereby compute
P^{-1}(G+e^{tad_{G}}F)
as follows:

\displaystyle P^{-1}(G+e^{tad_{G}}F)=\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{2abt}{abt^{2}-1}\\
\frac{abt\left(3abt-4a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(4abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ad\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)}{a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-abt+i\left(1-abt^{2}\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}\\
i\frac{abt\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)}{\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\end{matrix}\right].

(93)

On the other hand,

\displaystyle D=\textrm{diag}\left(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
8a^{2}b^{2}t^{4}-8abt^{2}-4t\sqrt{ab\left(abt^{2}-1\right)}\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)+1\\
8a^{2}b^{2}t^{4}-8abt^{2}+4t\sqrt{ab\left(abt^{2}-1\right)}\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)+1\end{bmatrix}\right).

(94)

Thus, we use (
94
) to calculate
\log(D)(D-I)^{-1}
by applying the series of matrix logarithm (
76
):

\log(D)(D-I)^{-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(I-D)^{j}}{j+1}\\
=\textrm{diag}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{4^{j}t^{j}}{j+1}(-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+2abt+\sqrt{ab(abt^{2}-1)}(2abt^{2}-1))^{j}}\\
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{4^{j}t^{j}}{j+1}(-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+2adt-\sqrt{ab(abt^{2}-1)}(2abt^{2}-1))^{j}}\end{bmatrix}.

(95)

Note that by assumption,
t\leq\eta<1/\sqrt{ab}
, which implies
t^{2}<1/ab
, or
abt^{2}-1<0
. Thus, (
95
) can be rewritten in terms of complex numbers:

\displaystyle\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}=\textrm{diag}\left(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{4^{j}t^{j}}{j+1}\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)^{j}}\\
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{4^{j}t^{j}}{j+1}\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)^{j}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=

\displaystyle-\textrm{diag}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-1\\
\frac{1}{4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)}\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]\\
\frac{1}{4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)}\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]\end{bmatrix}\right),

(96)

using the Taylor series for
-\log\left(1-x\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{j+1}}{j+1}=x\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{j}}{j+1}
. Note in particular that this means we are using the principal branch of the logarithm.

C.2.2
Simplification of the integrand

We now start substituting the variables we calculated into
Theorem
3
. We begin by multiplying (
96
) and (
93
) to get:

\displaystyle\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}P^{-1}(G+e^{t\mathrm{ad}_{G}}F)

\displaystyle=-\begin{bmatrix}\frac{2abt}{1-abt^{2}}\\
\frac{ab\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]}{4\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)}\frac{3abt-4a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}+i\left(4abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}{a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-abt+i\left(1-abt^{2}\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}}\\
i\frac{ab\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt-2a^{2}b^{2}t^{3}-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]}{4\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\end{bmatrix}.

(97)

The second, rightmost factor in the second row of (
97
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{4\left(1-abt^{2}\right)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}}.

(98)

Thus, we plug (
98
) back into (
97
) and simplify (
97
) to

\displaystyle\frac{ab}{4i\left(1-abt^{2}\right)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}}\left[\begin{matrix}-8it\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}\\
-\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt\left(1-abt^{2}\right)+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]\\
\log\left[1-4t\left(2abt\left(1-abt^{2}\right)-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)\sqrt{ab\left(1-abt^{2}\right)}\right)\right]\end{matrix}\right].

(99)

We now let
g(t)=\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}
. This allows us to rewrite (
92
) and (
99
) in a compact way:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}-\frac{1}{2bt}&\frac{-abt-ig\left(t\right)}{2b}&\frac{-abt+ig\left(t\right)}{2b}\\
-\frac{1}{2at}&\frac{-abt+ig\left(t\right)}{2a}&\frac{-abt-ig\left(t\right)}{2a}\\
1&1&1\end{matrix}\right]

(100)

\displaystyle\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}P^{-1}(G+e^{tab_{G}}F)

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{4ig^{3}(t)}\left[\begin{matrix}-8itg(t)\\
-\log\left[1-4t\left(2tg^{2}\left(t\right)+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)g\left(t\right)\right)\right]\\
\log\left[1-4t\left(2tg^{2}\left(t\right)-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)g\left(t\right)\right)\right]\end{matrix}\right].

(101)

Using (
100
) and (
101
) to assemble the entire integrand of (
89
), we have

\displaystyle P\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}P^{-1}(G+e^{tad_{G}}F)

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{4ig^{3}(t)}\left[\begin{matrix}-\frac{1}{2bt}&\frac{-abt-ig\left(t\right)}{2b}&\frac{-abt+ig\left(t\right)}{2b}\\
-\frac{1}{2at}&\frac{-abt+ig\left(t\right)}{2a}&\frac{-abt-ig\left(t\right)}{2a}\\
1&1&1\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}-8itg(t)\\
-\log\left[1-4t\left(2tg^{2}\left(t\right)+i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)g\left(t\right)\right)\right]\\
\log\left[1-4t\left(2tg^{2}\left(t\right)-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)g\left(t\right)\right)\right]\end{matrix}\right].

(102)

Let
z\left(t\right)\coloneqq abt+ig\left(t\right)
and
\nu\left(t\right)\coloneqq\log\left[1-4t\left(2tg^{2}\left(t\right)-i\left(2abt^{2}-1\right)g\left(t\right)\right)\right]
. Then, (
102
) implies that

\displaystyle P\log\left(D\right)\left(D-I\right)^{-1}P^{-1}(G+e^{tad_{G}}F)=\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{2g^{3}\left(t\right)}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2b}\Im\left\{z\left(t\right)\overline{\nu\left(t\right)}\right\}+\frac{2g(t)}{b}\\
\frac{1}{2a}\Im\left\{\overline{z\left(t\right)\nu\left(t\right)}\right\}+\frac{2g(t)}{a}\\
\Im\left\{\nu\left(t\right)\right\}-4tg(t)\end{matrix}\right],

(103)

where
\Im\left(c\right)
is the imaginary component of any
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

Noticing the recurrence of the expression in (
103
), we are motivated to expand out
\nu(t)
to

\displaystyle\nu\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left[1-8abt^{2}(1-abt^{2})+4it(2abt^{2}-1)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}\right].

(104)

By routine algebraic manipulations, we can show that the complex number within the logarithm of (
104
) has modulus one:

\displaystyle(1-8abt^{2}(1-abt^{2}))^{2}+(4t(2abt^{2}-1)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})})^{2}=1.

(105)

Thus, in light of (
104
) and (
105
), we can use Euler’s formula, double angle formulae, and then trigonometry to rewrite
\nu(t)
as the following:

\displaystyle\nu\left(t\right)=4i\arcsin(\sqrt{abt^{2}}).

(106)

Hence, by (
103
) and (
106
), we can simplify the integrand of (
89
) as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{\eta}\left[\begin{matrix}p^{2}&q^{2}&pq\end{matrix}\right]\int_{0}^{\eta}\frac{1}{2g^{3}\left(t\right)}\left[\begin{matrix}2at\arcsin(\sqrt{abt^{2}})+\frac{1}{b}2g(t)\\
2bt\arcsin(\sqrt{abt^{2}})+\frac{1}{a}2g(t)\\
-4\arcsin(\sqrt{abt^{2}})-4tg(t)\\
\end{matrix}\right]dt.

(107)

Finally, we split the integrand in (
107
) into the two terms shown in the vector and integrate them separately with the help of Wolfram Mathematica
[
89
]
. Doing so and adding together the results appropriately gives us the following:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{\eta}\left[\begin{matrix}p^{2}&q^{2}&pq\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{4ab^{2}\sqrt{ab(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\\
\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{4a^{2}b\sqrt{ab(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\\
-\frac{2ab\eta}{4}\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{a^{2}b^{2}\sqrt{ab(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\\
\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})(ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2abpq\eta).

(108)

When
ab<0
, we first show the following proper definition of inverse sine functions. Suppose we are interested in finding
y=\arcsin{ix}
, which we can write equivalently as follows:

\displaystyle y=\arcsin{ix}=i\arcsinh(-x).

Then the modified Hamiltonian takes the following form:

\displaystyle\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}(ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2abpq\eta)=\frac{\arcsinh(\sqrt{-ab\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{-ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}(ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2abpq\eta).

C.2.3
Convergence radius

The argument above is only valid if the conditions of Theorem
6
are actually satisfied. Thus, we need to ensure when
\left|\left|e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{G}}e^{t\cdot\mathrm{ad}_{F}}-I\right|\right|_{\textrm{op}}=\sigma_{\max}\left(D-I\right)<1
, or equivalently, when

\displaystyle\left|-8abt^{2}(1-abt^{2})-4it(2abt^{2}-1)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}\right|<1,

(109a)

\displaystyle\left|-8abt^{2}(1-abt^{2})+4it(2abt^{2}-1)\sqrt{ab(1-abt^{2})}\right|<1

(109b)

for all
t\in\left[0,\eta\right]
, as taken directly from from (
94
). Noting the equivalence of forms (
104
) and (
106
) for
\nu(t)
, (
109
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left|e^{\overline{\nu(t)}}-1\right|=\left|e^{-4i\arcsin z}-1\right|<1,

\displaystyle\left|e^{\nu(t)}-1\right|=\left|e^{4i\arcsin z}-1\right|<1,

respectively, where we have let
z\coloneqq\sqrt{abt^{2}}
. From here, we split the work into two cases based on the sign of
ab
.

Case 1:
ab\geq 0
.

In this case,
\arcsin z
is real, and since
\arcsin
only takes arguments from
-1
to
1
, we get a contradiction when
z>1
. Since
e^{-4i\arcsin z}=\overline{e^{4i\arcsin z}}
,
|e^{-4i\arcsin z}-1|<1
if and only if
|e^{4i\arcsin z}|<1
. When
0<4\arcsin{z}<\frac{\pi}{3}
, we have that
|e^{4i\arcsin z}-1|<1
. Similarly, when
\frac{5\pi}{3}<4\arcsin{z}<2\pi
,
|e^{4i\arcsin z}-1|<1
again. Thus, the absolute convergence criteria in Theorem
6
is satisfied whenever
z\in\left(0,\sin(\frac{\pi}{12})\right)\cup\left(\sin(\frac{5\pi}{12}),1\right)
. We now show that this actually implies that the modified Hamiltonian (
20
) is absolutely convergent whenever
|z|\in(0,1)
.

For any
\eta\in(0,\sqrt{\frac{1}{ab}})
, we pick
\eta^{\prime}=\max(\sqrt{\sin{\frac{11\pi}{24}}},\eta)
. By construction
\eta^{\prime}\in\left(\sin(\frac{5\pi}{12}),1\right)
and thus the series (
20
) converges absolutely for
\eta^{\prime}
. We now compare the terms of the two series:

\left|\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}\right|\\
\leq\left|\frac{(\eta^{\prime})^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}\right|.

(110)

By the dominated convergence theorem, the modified Hamiltonian (
20
) converges absolutely for
\eta
chosen above.

Case 2:
ab<0
.

When
ab<0
, we let
F^{\prime}=|a|p^{2},G^{\prime}=|b|q^{2}
. From Appendix
C.1
, we know that
\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)
is always a monomial of
p,q
and
a,b
, regardless of the signs of
p
and
q
. We have the following equality:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)\right|}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left|\left\{G^{\prime r_{1}}F^{\prime s_{1}}\cdots G^{\prime r_{n}}F^{\prime s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)\right|}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left|\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left\{G^{\prime r_{1}}F^{\prime s_{1}}\cdots G^{\prime r_{n}}F^{\prime s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)}{n\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}\right|.

Since the modified Hamiltonian (
20
) converges absolutely whenever
\sqrt{|a||b|t^{2}}<1
, it converges absolutely for
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
. Since the absolute value of the terms is the same, the convergence is also absolute when
ab<0
, but except when
\sqrt{|ab|t^{2}}<1
.

Figure
2
shows the graph of the function
\eta\mapsto\frac{\arcsin\left(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}}\right)}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}
that appears in the final expression (
108
) of the modified Hamiltonian. Note that the Taylor expansion of
\eta\mapsto\frac{\arcsin\left(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}}\right)}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}
is as follows
[
89
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\arcsin\left(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}}\right)}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}=1+\frac{2(\eta\sqrt{ab})^{2}}{3}+\frac{8(\eta\sqrt{ab})^{4}}{15}+\frac{16(\eta\sqrt{ab})^{6}}{35}+\frac{128(\eta\sqrt{ab})^{8}}{215}+\cdots,

which is precisely what the simplified series formula (
87
) for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
suggests. One may further check that the same expansion holds when
ab<0
.

Figure 2
:
The function
\eta\mapsto\frac{\arcsin\left(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}}\right)}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}
with different choices of
a
and
b
.

C.3
Verification: Hamiltonian flow of modified Hamiltonian

In this subsection, we verify Theorem
2
in the single-variable quadratic case. To this end, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 10
.

Define the modified Hamiltonian as

\tilde{H}_{\eta}(p,q)=\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}(ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2abpq\eta).

If
\left(p(t),q(t)\right)
follows the Hamiltonian flow of the modified Hamiltonian:

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}p(t)\\
q(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}(p(t),q(t))\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\widetilde{H}_{\eta}(p(t),q(t))\end{pmatrix},

and starts at

\begin{pmatrix}p(0)\\
q(0)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}\\
q_{0}\end{pmatrix},

then we have:

\begin{pmatrix}p(\eta)\\
q(\eta)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2b\eta\\
2a\eta&1-4ab\eta^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}\\
q_{0}\end{pmatrix},

which coincides with the iterates of symplectic Euler (
5
) when
H(p,q)=ap^{2}+bq^{2}
.

Proof.

For
\eta>0
, let

C_{\eta}=\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}

so that the modified Hamiltonian above becomes:

\tilde{H}_{\eta}(p,q)=C_{\eta}(ap^{2}+bq^{2}-2abpq\eta).

The Hamiltonian flow is:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}p(t)\\
q(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2C_{\eta}b(-q(t)+a\eta p(t))\\
2C_{\eta}a(p(t)-b\eta q(t))\end{pmatrix}=2C_{\eta}\begin{pmatrix}ab\eta&-b\\
a&-ab\eta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p(t)\\
q(t)\end{pmatrix}.

(111)

Using the matrix exponential, the solution to the Hamiltonian flow (
111
) at time
t=\eta
is:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p(\eta)\\
q(\eta)\end{pmatrix}=\exp\left(2\eta C_{\eta}\begin{pmatrix}ab\eta&-b\\
a&-ab\eta\end{pmatrix}\right)\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}\\
q_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

(112)

We compute the matrix exponential in (
112
) as follows:

\displaystyle\exp\left(2\eta C_{\eta}\begin{pmatrix}ab\eta&-b\\
a&-ab\eta\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}E_{11}&E_{12}\\
E_{21}&E_{22}\end{bmatrix},

where the coefficients are as follows:

\displaystyle E_{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(ab\eta+\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\right)\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\left(ab\eta-\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\right)\exp\left(-2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}

\displaystyle E_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{b}\exp\left(-2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{a(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}-\frac{\sqrt{b}\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{a(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}

\displaystyle E_{21}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{a}\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{b(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}-\frac{\sqrt{a}\exp\left(-2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{b(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}

\displaystyle E_{22}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(-ab\eta+\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\right)\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\left(-ab\eta-\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\right)\exp\left(-2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)}{2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}}.

To simplify the coefficients above, we let
S=\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}
,
T=ab\eta^{2}
. We further calculate:

\displaystyle\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(2\sqrt{ab(ab\eta^{2}-1)}\eta\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(2i\arcsin\left(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=1-2T+2iS

Similarly, we have:

\displaystyle\exp\left(-2\sqrt{ab\left(ab\eta^{2}-1\right)}\eta C_{\eta}\right)=1-2T-2iS.

Hence, we could simplify the coefficients in the following way:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}E_{11}&E_{12}\\
E_{21}&E_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2b\eta\\
2a\eta&1-4ab\eta^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

The symplectic Euler algorithm, (
5
), in this case is:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p_{\text{new}}\\
q_{\text{new}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}-\eta(2bq_{0})\\
q_{0}+\eta(2ap_{\text{new}})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}-2b\eta q_{0}\\
q_{0}+2a\eta\left(p_{0}-2b\eta q_{0}\right)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2b\eta\\
2a\eta&1-4ab\eta^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_{0}\\
q_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Since the coefficients match, we confirm that the iterations are indeed interpolated by the Hamiltonian flow generated by the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
.
∎

C.4
The multivariate case

In this subsection we derive the closed-form of the modified Hamiltonian in the multivariate quadratic case by utilizing results from monovariate case.

The general idea of the proof is the following: we first derive connections between the iterated Poisson brackets in multivariate and univariate cases. We then reorder the series form (
20
) of the modified Hamiltonian in the multivariate case
\widetilde{H}^{F,G}_{\eta}(p,q)
and relate the reordered series with the series form of the modified Hamiltonian in the univariate case to get closed-form expression of
\widetilde{H}^{F,G}_{\eta}(p,q)
. Furthermore, this allows us to determine the absolute convergence radius of the reordered form, which then implies equivalence between the original form and the reordered form.

C.4.1
Notations and settings

We assume that
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d\geq 2
. We assume that
B,C\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are symmetric matrices such that
BC
is positive semi-definite. Let
Q^{-1}\Lambda Q
be the diagonalization of
BC
. We define
F(p)=p^{\top}Bp
,
G(q)=q^{\top}Cq
, and
H^{F,G}(p,q)=F(p)+G(q)
. In using results from last subsection, we assume that
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
, and define
f(u)=au^{2},g(v)=bv^{2}
, where
ab>0
, and let
H^{f,g}(u,v)=f(u)+g(v)
.

We want to find the closed-form expression of modified Hamiltonian for the following symplectic Euler updates:

\displaystyle p_{k+1}=p_{k}-2\eta Cq_{k},\quad q_{k+1}=q_{k}+2\eta Bp_{k+1}

Throughout the subsection, we let
\star
denote the tuple
(r_{1},s_{1},\dots,r_{n},s_{n})
, i.e.,

\star\coloneqq(r_{1},s_{1},\dots,r_{n},s_{n}).

We denote the rank (for its definition, check the review on iterated Poisson bracket in Section
6
) of the Poisson bracket corresponding to the tuple
\star
with
\mathcal{N}(\star)
, denote
\mathcal{M}(\star)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}(r_{i}+s_{i}),
and denote the set of tuples of rank
N
with
\mathcal{S}(N)
. Finally, we define the following function to express the constant coefficient in the
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
series:

\displaystyle\tau(\star)=\tau((r_{1},s_{1},\cdots,r_{n},s_{n}))={\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}{{\frac{1}{\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}

For simplicity of notations, we define

\displaystyle I_{p,q}^{F,G}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)

\displaystyle I_{u,v}^{f,g}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{g^{r_{1}}f^{s_{1}}g^{r_{2}}f^{s_{2}}\cdots g^{r_{n}}f^{s_{n}}\right\}(u,v)

Results from Appendix
C.1
imply that
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
must be a monomial containing
a,b,u,v
. We denote the constant factor within
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
with
J(\star)
, denote the power of
a
within
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
with
L_{a}(\star)
and the power of
b
within
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
with
L_{b}(\star)
. The following proposition is a direct consequence of our work above:

Proposition 1
.

I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
must be one of the following forms:

•

J(\star)a^{L_{a}(\star)}b^{L_{b}(\star)}u^{2}
,

•

J(\star)a^{L_{a}(\star)}b^{L_{b}(\star)}v^{2}
, or

•

J(\star)a^{L_{a}(\star)}b^{L_{b}(\star)}uv
.

We define the following subsets of possible tuples:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{pp}(n)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\star\in\mathcal{S}(n)\mid I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)\text{ contains }uu\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{qq}(n)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\star\in\mathcal{S}(n)\mid I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)\text{ contains }uu\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{pq}(n)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\star\in\mathcal{S}(n)\mid I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)\text{ contains }uv\}

Notice that
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)\cup\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)\cup\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)
implies that
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)\neq 0
.

Finally, we define the following functions to connect multivariate and univariate quadratic cases.

\displaystyle T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\lambda)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}&\lambda>0,\\
\frac{\arcsinh(\sqrt{-\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{-\lambda\eta^{2}(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}&\lambda<0.\end{cases},\quad T^{\text{cross}}_{\eta}(\lambda)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{-2\sqrt{\lambda}\arcsin(\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}&\lambda>0,\\
\frac{2\sqrt{-\lambda}\arcsinh(\sqrt{-\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}&\lambda<0.\end{cases}

C.4.2
Connections between the iterated Poisson brackets in two cases

Lemma 11
.

If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
, then
L_{a}(\star)=L_{b}(\star)+1=\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}
. If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
, then
L_{b}(\star)=L_{a}(\star)+1=\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}
. If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)
, then
L_{a}(\star)=L_{b}(\star)=\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}
.

Proof.

This can be verified via an inductive argument based on
\mathcal{M}(\star)=\{1,2,3,\cdots\}
. As the base cases, the proposed lemma holds for
M=1
, where
f=au^{2}
,
g=bv^{2}
and
M=2
, where
\{f,g\}=-4abuv=-\{g,f\}
. Suppose that the lemma holds for any tuples
\star
such that
\mathcal{M}(\star)\leq n
, we verify that it also holds for any tuples
\star
such that
\mathcal{M}(\star)\leq n+1
. For the sake of simplicity we only verify the case where
\{\cdot,f\}
is applied to
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
. We denote the new tuple in this case as
\star^{\prime}
and remark that
\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})=\mathcal{M}(\star)+1
.

•

If
