I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}u^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}u^{2},au^{2}\}

\displaystyle=0.

•

If
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}v^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}v^{2},au^{2}\}

\displaystyle=J(\star^{\prime})a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}uv

\displaystyle=J(\star^{\prime})a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}uv.

•

If
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}uv
, then

\displaystyle\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}uv,au^{2}\}

\displaystyle=J(\star^{\prime})a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+2}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}u^{2}

\displaystyle=J(\star^{\prime})a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}u^{2}.

Thus, the induction argument is verified in all cases.
∎

Lemma 12
.

If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}Bp
.
If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)q^{\top}C(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}q
.
If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}q
.

Proof.

This can be verified using an inductive argument based on
\mathcal{M}(\star)=\{1,2,3,\cdots\}
. As the base case, the proposed lemma holds for
M=1
, where
F=p^{\top}Bp
,
G=q^{\top}Cq
and
M=2
, where
\{F,G\}=-4p^{\top}BCq=-\{G,F\}
. Suppose the lemma holds for any tuples
\star
such that
\mathcal{M}(\star)\leq n
, we verify that it also holds for any tuples
\star
such that
\mathcal{M}(\star)\leq n+1
. For the sake of simplicity we only verify the case where
\{\cdot,F\}
is applied to
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)
and when
\{\cdot,f\}
is applied to
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
. To show the inductive argument, we need to compare
\{I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star),F\}
and
\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}
.

If
\star
ends with
\{\dots,r_{n},0\}
where
r_{n}>0
, the new tuple
\star^{\prime}
will be
\{\dots,r_{n},1\}
. On the other hand, if
\star
ends with
\{\dots,r_{n},s_{n}\}
where
r_{n},s_{n}>0
, the new tuple
\star^{\prime}
will be
\{\dots,r_{n},s_{n}+1\}
. In both cases,
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star^{\prime})={I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star),F}
. Note that
\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})=\mathcal{M}(\star)+1
.

When
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
,
I_{p,q}^{F,G}=J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}Bp
and
I_{u,v}^{f,g}=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}u^{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\{I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star),F\}=\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}=0.

When
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{vv}(n)
,
I_{p,q}^{F,G}=J(\star)q^{\top}C(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}q
and
I_{u,v}^{f,g}=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}v^{2}
. In this case,

\displaystyle\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}v^{2},au^{2}\}

\displaystyle=4J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}uv

\displaystyle=4J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}uv.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\{I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star),F\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)q^{\top}C(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}q,p^{\top}Bp\}

\displaystyle=4J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+1}{2}}q

\displaystyle=4J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})}{2}}q.

Since the constant term match and the power of
a
,
b
matches with that of
B
and
C
, the assumption is verified.

When
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)
,
I_{p,q}^{F,G}=J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}q
and
I_{u,v}^{f,g}=J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}uv
. In this case,

\displaystyle\{I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star),f\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}uv,au^{2}\}

\displaystyle=2J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)+2}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}u^{2}

\displaystyle=2J(\star)a^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})+1}{2}}b^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})-1}{2}}u^{2}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\{I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star),F\}

\displaystyle=\{J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}q,p^{\top}Bp\}

\displaystyle=2J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}Bp

\displaystyle=2J(\star)p^{\top}(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star^{\prime})-1}{2}}Bp

Since the constant term match and the power of
a
,
b
matches with that of
B
and
C
, the assumption is verified.
∎

Corollary 4
.

If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)(Qp)^{\top}\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}QBp
.
If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{vv}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)(QC^{\top}q)^{\top}\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}Qq
.
If
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(n)
, then
I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star)=J(\star)(Qp)^{\top}\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}Qq
.

Proof.

We note that
(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}B=(Q^{-1}\Lambda Q)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}B=Q^{-1}\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}QB
,
C(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}=\\
C(Q^{-1}\Lambda Q)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}=CQ^{-1}\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}Q
, and
(BC)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}=(Q^{-1}\Lambda Q)^{\frac{M(\star)}{2}}=Q^{-1}\Lambda^{\frac{M(\star)}{2}}Q
.
∎

This confirms that if
\star\in\mathcal{S}_{pp}
, then
I_{u,v}^{f,g}(\star)
is a bilinear product of a matrix with
p,p
. Similar properties hold for
\mathcal{S}_{qq}
and
\mathcal{S}_{pq}
.

C.4.3
Reordering the series: finding the closed-form and absolute convergence radius

Lemma 13
.

The Dynkin form (
20
) of the modified Hamiltonian in multivariate quadratic case
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)
can be rearranged into the following:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)

\displaystyle=(Qp)^{\top}\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{pp}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}\right)QBp

\displaystyle\quad+(QC^{\top}q)^{\top}\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{qq}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}\right)Qq

\displaystyle\quad+(Qp)^{\top}\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{pq}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)\Lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}\right)Qq

(116)

Note that the equivalence between
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)
and
\hat{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)
is not guaranteed, because we have not proven the absolute convergence yet.

Proof.

Let us start from the Dynkin form (
20
) of the modified Hamiltonian:

\widetilde{H}^{F,G}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\end{subarray}}{\frac{\eta^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}(p,q)}{\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

The summation can be reordered as:

\hat{H}^{F,G}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{pp}(n)\cup\mathcal{S}_{pq}(n)\cup\mathcal{S}_{pq}(n)}\tau(\star)\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)}I_{p,q}^{F,G}(\star).

We compute the summation for
\mathcal{S}_{pp}(n),\mathcal{S}_{qq}(n)
and
\mathcal{S}_{pq}(n)
separately, and apply the results in Lemma
12
to conclude.
∎

Theorem 7
.

The reordered
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
series in Lemma
13
has the following closed form:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)

\displaystyle=(Qp)^{\top}T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\Lambda)QBp

\displaystyle\quad+(QC^{\top}q)^{\top}T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\Lambda)Qq

\displaystyle\quad+(Qp)^{\top}T^{\text{cross}}_{\eta}(\Lambda)Qq.

Whenever each eigenvalue
\lambda
in
\Lambda
satisfies that
|\lambda\eta^{2}|\leq 1
, the original BCH series
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)
converges absolutely and reordering of summation is allowed. Since
BC
is assumed to be diagonalizable, if the maximal singular value of
BC
is smaller than
\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}
, then
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)=\hat{H}_{\eta}^{F,G}(p,q)
.

Proof.

Theorem
3
says that in univariate case, BCH series
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{f,g}(u,v)
converges to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})(au^{2}+bv^{2}-2abuv\eta)
. Furthermore, the convergence is absolute whenever
|ab\eta^{2}|<1
.

Assume without loss of generality that
ab>0
, for the other case is similar. Absolute convergence allows for the following reordering of the summation:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{f,g}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)(ab)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}\right)au^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{vv}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)(ab)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}\right)bv^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)(ab)^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}\right)uv

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}(1-ab\eta^{2})}}\arcsin(\sqrt{ab\eta^{2}})(au^{2}+bu^{2}-2abuv\eta).

(117)

We now substitute the variable
ab
with
\lambda
. Equation (
117
) above implies the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uu}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)\lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}=T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\lambda)

(118a)

\displaystyle\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{vv}(N)}\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}\tau(\star)J(\star)\lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\arcsin(\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}=T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\lambda)

(118b)

\displaystyle\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{\star\in\mathcal{S}_{uv}(N)}\frac{\eta^{\mathcal{M}(\star)-1}}{\mathcal{M}(\star)}\tau(\star)J(\star)\lambda^{\frac{\mathcal{M}(\star)}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2\sqrt{\lambda}\arcsin(\sqrt{\lambda\eta^{2}})}{\sqrt{(1-\lambda\eta^{2})}}=T^{\text{cross}}_{\eta}(\lambda).

(118c)

Furthermore, the absolute convergence of original Dynkin series (
20
) when
|\lambda\eta^{2}|<1
implies absolute convergence of
T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\lambda)
and
T^{\text{cross}}_{\eta}(\lambda)
when
|\lambda\eta^{2}|<1
.

Since
\Lambda
is a diagonal matrix, taking powers of
\Lambda
is equivalent with taking the same powers element-wise. Thus, the summation of matrices in (
116
) is equivalent with summing each element along the diagonal separately. Furthermore, since the terms in (
116
) are precisely those in (
117
) and thereby equal to the respective ones in (
118
), the closed form should equal to the application of
T_{\eta}^{\text{pure}}
and
T_{\eta}^{\text{cross}}
on the diagonal elements separately. Finally, note that since
T^{\text{cross}}_{\eta}(\lambda)=|\lambda|T^{\text{pure}}_{\eta}(\lambda)
holds for both
\lambda>0
and
\lambda<0
, we could simplify
T_{\eta}^{\text{cross}}(\Lambda)
as
|\Lambda|T_{\eta}^{\text{pure}}(\Lambda)
. Thus, the closed-form is proven.

Finally, following the convergence criteria in Theorem
3
, the convergence criteria here should be:

\lambda_{\max}(|\Lambda|)\eta^{2}=\sigma_{\max}(BC)<1.

Since
\sigma_{\max}(BC)\leq\sigma_{\max}(B)\sigma_{\max}(C)
, one sufficient convergence criterion is:

\sigma_{\max}(B)\sigma_{\max}(C)\eta^{2}<1.

∎

Appendix D
Absolute convergence of the modified Hamiltonian

Much of the existing literature makes references to the convergence of the modified Hamiltonian
[
35
,
4
,
31
]
, as from a theoretical perspective, convergence of the Dynkin series (
20
) is necessary to define a modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
in terms of the Dynkin series which is a well-defined function from
\mathcal{Z}
to
\mathbb{R}
. In general, it is well-known that if the symplectic Euler method (
5
) is nonlinear, the Dynkin formula does not converge for
any

\eta
—see, e.g.,
[
71
]
,
[
31
]
, or Sections IX.3 and IX.9.3 from
[
35
]
. However, numerical simulations—e.g., those shown in
[
85
]
and Chapters I, IX, and X from
[
35
]
—suggest that for even non-smooth, closed, proper convex functions
F
and
G
, the orbits generated by the iterations (
5
) lie on a closed, bounded orbit for
\eta
sufficiently small. This at least suggests that the results shown above on the existence of closed, bounded orbits for small enough
\eta>0
in the quadratic and logarithm cases also exist for larger classes of functions.

That being said, the existence of closed, bounded orbits which look like the level set of some function does not necessarily imply that the BCH formula as it stands in either its Dynkin or integral form (i.e., without renormalization or analytic continuation) converges to a function whose level sets match with those orbits. In fact, there are direct counter-examples to the latter when
F
is quadratic and
G
is a polynomial with order higher than 3, and
d=1
. Numerical simulations—e.g., those in Figure
3
—imply the existence of bounded, closed orbits for small enough
\eta
and any initial condition throughout
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, even though
[
73
,
31
]
show that the BCH formula does not converge in such cases for
any

\eta>0
when
G
is a polynomial of degree
\geq 3
. Note that the smaller circles in the first plot of Figure
3
of the same color are suborbital trajectories traced out on smaller portions of the phase space than the larger orbit for a strict subset of the iterations. For instance, for the trajectory in blue corresponding to
\left(p_{0},q_{0}\right)=\left(2,3\right)
, there are
26
smaller circles. Thus, if we consider the dynamics of every 26th iterate of symplectic Euler (
5
), then these are still periodic and lie on one of the smaller circles.

(a)

H(x,y)=x^{1.5}+y^{1.5},\eta=1

(b)

H(x,y)=x^{2}+y^{2},\eta=1

(c)

H(x,y)=x^{4}+y^{4},\eta=1

Figure 3
:
Trajectories of iterations symplectic Euler discretizations of the Hamiltonian flows plotted in scatter plot.
Different colors correspond to different initial positions.

(a)
H(x,y)=x^{1.5}+y^{1.5},\eta=1
(b)
H(x,y)=x^{2}+y^{2},\eta=1
(c)
H(x,y)=x^{4}+y^{4},\eta=1

At least to the authors’ knowledge, no prior study other than
[
73
,
31
]
has tried to study which general classes of functions
F
and
G
will or will not lead to a convergent modified Hamiltonian, expressed with the Dynkin formula. But more generally, in the field of Lie algebra, there are numerous studies which state and prove sufficient criteria for the Dynkin formula to converge for two elements
x,y
in a normed Lie algebra
\left(L,\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|\right)

[
15
,
14
,
74
,
20
]
. These studies require a coercivity bound of the form
\left|\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|\right|\leq\mu\left|\left|x\right|\right|\left|\left|y\right|\right|
for some
\mu\geq 0
. To apply these results directly to the Poisson algebra over
C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
, we would require at least something of the form
\left|\left|\left\{F,G\right\}\right|\right|=\left|\left|\nabla_{q}F\cdot\nabla_{p}G-\nabla_{p}F\cdot\nabla_{q}G\right|\right|\leq\mu\left|\left|F\right|\right|\left|\left|G\right|\right|
for some suitable function norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|
and
\mu\geq 0
, e.g., some
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
norm or Hölder space norm
[
29
,
47
,
45
]
. However, this effectively amounts to enforcing a reverse Poincaré inequality over the function space in question, or equivalently, that the derivative map is bounded over the function space in question, which we would only generally expect to be true over a finite-dimensional function space
[
47
,
66
]
. There are a few known examples of this, e.g., the quadratic case discussed in Section
8
and Appendix
C
. In such cases, it would be most straightforward to find a basis for the
finite
-dimensional function space
\mathcal{F}
such that the Poisson bracket can be expressed as a matrix acting on this basis.

To avoid the aforementioned issues in imposing that the Poisson bracket is bounded (or similarly, that the derivative operator is bounded) over the underlying function space, we derive criteria for convergence that rely on imposing growth conditions on the IPBs of
F
and
G
instead of directly bounding the Poisson bracket or derivative operator. Furthermore, we assume absolute convergence, since doing so both allows us to reorder the terms in the Dynkin series (
20
) as needed
[
68
]
and allows us to bound the absolute value of the IPBs (
6
) in each term, as shown in the start of Appendix
D.1
.

We consider the following two growth assumptions on the IPBs in the Dynkin series (
20
):

1.

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|
is bounded above by a function of
k\coloneqq r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}
only, and

2.

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|
is bounded above by the product of a function of
k
and
\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}
.

In either case, the bound may also depend on
\left(p,q\right)
, in which case
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is defined as a pointwise limit function at each
\left(p,q\right)
separately but is not necessarily continuous. However, if the bound holds uniformly for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, then by the Weierstrass M-test
[
68
]
,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is also continuous on all of
\mathcal{Z}
.

Case 1

In this case, we assume a uniform bound on the IPBs which is a function of the “order” of the IPB—i.e., we assume that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|\leq A\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)

(119)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
, where
A:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Doing so in general gives Lemma
15
, from which we can derive two corollaries upon further assumptions for the function
A
which use explicitly verifiable bounds on the size of
\eta
.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that there exist mappings
b,c:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\\
b\left(p,q\right)\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)^{2}c\left(p,q\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}-1}

(120)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and integer pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\eta<\log 2/\left(2c\left(p,q\right)\right)
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\frac{2b\left(p,q\right)e^{2\eta c\left(p,q\right)}}{2-e^{2\eta c\left(p,q\right)}}.

(121)

Proof.

Replace
A
with (
120
) in Lemma
15
. Then, we note that the exponential generating function of the Fubini numbers is
[
64
]

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{a_{n}\frac{x^{n}}{n!}}=\frac{1}{2-e^{x}},

which converges only when
x<\log 2
. Simplifying the result, we get (
121
).
∎

Theorem 9
.

Suppose that there exist mappings
b,c:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and some
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\\
b\left(p,q\right)\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)^{2-t}c\left(p,q\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}

(122)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and integer pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\eta<\log 2/\left(2c\left(p,q\right)\right)
,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)
is well-defined and bounded, and moreover,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)
is continuous on every open subset of
\mathcal{Z}
where the upper bound (
122
) holds uniformly for all
\left(p,q\right)
in that open set. Moreover, the following bound holds for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
:

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\frac{2b\left(p,q\right)}{\eta}\left(\textrm{Li}_{t}\left(\frac{2\eta c\left(p,q\right)}{\log 2}\right)-\eta\right),

where
\textrm{Li}_{t}
is the polylogarithm of order
t

[
84
,
2
]
.

Proof.

We replace
A
with (
122
) in Lemma
15
, thereby giving us

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq b\left(p,q\right)\left(4\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k-1}c\left(p,q\right)^{k}a_{k-1}}{k^{\top}\left(k-1\right)!}}-2\right).

(123)

We have the following upper bound for the Fubini numbers from
[
93
]
:

\displaystyle a_{k-1}<\frac{\left(k-1\right)!}{\left(\log 2\right)^{k}},

(124)

and using (
124
) to bound (
123
) from above,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq 2b\left(p,q\right)\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{k^{\top}}\left(\frac{2\eta c\left(p,q\right)}{\log 2}\right)^{k}}-1\right).

(125)

By the definition of polylogarithm
[
84
,
2
]
, the result follows.
∎

Case 2

We now assume that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|\leq B\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}

(126)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
, where
B:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Doing so gives the more general convergence criteria stated as Lemma
16
, and we can also derive a corollary from Lemma
16
upon further assumptions on the function
B
.

Theorem 10
.

Suppose that there exist mappings
b,c:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and some
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\\
b\left(p,q\right)\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)^{1-t}c\left(p,q\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}

(127)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and integer pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\eta<\left(2-\sqrt{3}\right)/c\left(p,q\right)
,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)
is well-defined and bounded, and moreover,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is continuous on every open subset of
\mathcal{Z}
where the upper bound (
127
) holds uniformly for all
\left(p,q\right)
in that open set. Moreover, the following bound holds for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
:

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\frac{b\left(p,q\right)}{\eta}\textrm{Li}_{t}\left(\eta\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)c\left(p,q\right)\right),

where
\textrm{Li}_{t}
is the polylogarithm of order
t
.

D.1
Proof of the two convergence criteria

For each case, we re-sum the modified Hamiltonian (
20
) such that the outermost summation sums over consecutive orders of
\eta
. Doing so also gives each order-by-order correction in
\eta
to the modified Hamiltonian and a way to truncate the modified Hamiltonian at a given order.

Using the notations introduced in Section
6
and the double summation identity
\sum_{n=m}^{N}{\sum_{k=n}^{N}{a_{nk}}}=\sum_{k=m}^{N}{\sum_{n=m}^{k}{a_{nk}}}
, for any
m,N\in\mathbb{Z}
with
m\leq N

[
33
]
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(N-1\right)}\left(p,q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}\sum_{k=n}^{N}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}}{k\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

(128)

Hence, taking
N\rightarrow\infty
in (
128
) and assuming absolute convergence, (
20
) is equal to

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

(129)

Since the steps above are reversible, in practice, once establishing absolute convergence of (
129
), we can reorder the terms back to establish absolute convergence for the original modified Hamiltonian (
20
). Moreover, by looking at (
128
), we observe that the order
\left(N-1\right)
correction in the modified Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{N-1}}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\frac{\left(-1\right)^{n-1}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=N\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}.

(130)

By applying the triangle inequality to (
129
), we start out with

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}

(131)

in either case. From here, the game is to bound the inner two sums (i.e., (
130
)) as tightly as is reasonably possible while still giving us a tractable expression, and then check the outermost sum for convergence.

Case 1

Given the upper bound (
119
) in case 1, (
131
) can be bounded from above like

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}A\left(k\right)}{k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}}}.

(132)

Rather surprisingly, we can express the innermost sum in (
132
) in closed form, as formalized in the lemma below:

Lemma 14
.

For all
n,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
k\geq n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{k!}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}}}=n!2^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n},

(133)

where
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n}
is a Stirling number of the second kind—i.e., the number of ways to partition a set of
k
objects into
n
non-empty subsets
[
33
]
.

Proof.

By the residue theorem
[
46
]
,
\oint_{\left|z\right|=1}{\frac{1}{z^{1+p}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}}=\delta_{p0}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta
[
83
]
. Thus, using properties of the Kronecker delta, we can simplify the left side of (
133
) as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{k!}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}}=k!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}\geq 1\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}\geq 1\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{1}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}}

\displaystyle=k!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}\geq 1\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}\geq 1\end{subarray}}{\frac{\delta_{\left(k-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\left(r_{m}+s_{m}\right)}\right)0}}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}}=k!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{j}+s_{j}\geq 1\\
j=1,\dots,n\end{subarray}}{\frac{1}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}\oint_{\left|z\right|=1}{\frac{1}{z^{k+1-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\left(r_{m}+s_{m}\right)}}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}}}

\displaystyle=k!\oint_{\left|z\right|=1}{\frac{1}{z^{k+1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{j}+s_{j}\geq 1\\
j=1,\dots,n\end{subarray}}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{\frac{z^{r_{m}}z^{s_{m}}}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}}=k!\oint_{\left|z\right|=1}{\frac{1}{z^{k+1}}\left(\sum_{r+s\geq 1}{\frac{z^{r}z^{s}}{r!s!}}\right)^{n}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}}.

(134)

The sum in (
134
) can be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{r+s\geq 1}{\frac{z^{r}z^{s}}{r!s!}}=\sum_{r+s=1}^{\infty}{\frac{z^{r+s}}{r!s!}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\sum_{r+s=j}{\frac{z^{j}}{r!s!}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{z^{j}}{j!}\sum_{r+s=j}{\frac{j!}{r!s!}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{z^{j}}{j!}\sum_{s=0}^{j}{\binom{j}{s}}}.

(135)

Then, using the binomial theorem and the exponential series
e^{x}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{x^{j}/j!}

[
2
]
to simplify (
135
),

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{z^{j}}{j!}2^{j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(2z\right)^{j}}{j!}}=e^{2z}-\frac{\left(2z\right)^{0}}{0!}=e^{2z}-1.

(136)

Substituting (
136
) into (
134
) and applying Cauchy’s differentiation formula
[
46
]
, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\frac{k!}{\prod_{m=1}^{n}{r_{m}!s_{m}!}}}=\frac{k!}{2\pi i}\oint_{\left|z\right|=1}{\frac{\left(e^{2z}-1\right)^{n}}{z^{k+1}}dz}=\frac{d^{(k)}}{dz^{(k)}}\left[\left(e^{2z}-1\right)^{n}\right]_{z=0}.

(137)

Finally, using the binomial theorem, exponential series, and then the definition of Stirling numbers of the second kind
[
33
]
to expand (
137
),

\displaystyle\left(e^{2z}-1\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{n}{\binom{n}{l}\left(e^{2z}\right)^{n-l}\left(-1\right)^{l}}=\sum_{l=0}^{n}{\binom{n}{l}e^{2z\left(n-l\right)}\left(-1\right)^{l}}.

This implies for all
k>0
:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{(k)}}{dz^{(k)}}\left[\left(e^{2z}-1\right)^{n}\right]_{z=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{n}{\binom{n}{l}\frac{d^{(k)}}{dz^{(k)}}\left.e^{2z\left(n-l\right)}\right|_{z=0}\left(-1\right)^{l}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{n}{\binom{n}{l}2^{k}\left(n-l\right)^{k}\left.e^{2z\left(n-l\right)}\right|_{z=0}\left(-1\right)^{l}}

\displaystyle=n!2^{k}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{l=0}^{n}{\left(-1\right)^{l}\binom{n}{l}\left(n-l\right)^{k}}

\displaystyle=n!2^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n},

as we wanted to show.
∎

With Lemma
14
proven, we can now proceed with the proof of Lemma
15
.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose that there exists some
A:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq A\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n},p,q\right)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and integer pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k+1}A\left(k,p,q\right)a_{k-1}}{k^{2}\left(k-1\right)!}}-2A\left(1,p,q\right),

(138)

where
a_{k-1}
is the
(k-1)
-th Fubini number
[
93
,
43
]
(also known as ordered Bell number
[
75
]
) for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
14
and properties of Stirling numbers of the second kind
[
33
]
to simplify (
132
),

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}A\left(k\right)}{k!k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{n!2^{k}}{n}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k}A\left(k\right)}{k!k}\left(\left(k-1\right)!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k}+\sum_{n=1}^{k-1}{\left(n-1\right)!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k}A\left(k\right)}{k!k}\left[\left(k-1\right)!+\sum_{n=1}^{k-1}{\left(n-1\right)!\left(n\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n}+\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n-1}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k}A\left(k\right)}{k!k}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k-1}{n!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n}}+\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\left(n-1\right)!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n-1}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}2^{k}A\left(k\right)}{k!k}\left(2\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}{m!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{m}}-\delta_{(k-1)0}\right)}.

(139)

In the above, the step
(i)
follows from the identities
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k}=1
and
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{n}=n\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n}+\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{n-1}
;
while the step
(ii)
follows from
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k-1}{0}=\delta_{(k-1)0}
.
Finally, the Fubini numbers
a_{n}
can be computed from the Stirling numbers of the second kind via the formula
[
64
,
43
]

\displaystyle a_{n}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}{m!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{m}}\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(140)

Substituting (
140
) into (
139
), we are done.
∎

Case 2

Lemma 16
.

Suppose that there exists some
B:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq B\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n},p,q\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}{r_{i}!s_{i}!}

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and integer pairs
r_{i}+s_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(\eta\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)\right)^{k}B\left(k,p,q\right)}{k^{2}}}<\infty,

the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)
converges. Moreover, the following bounds hold for all
\left(p,q\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
:

\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k,p,q\right)}{k^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{2\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k,p,q\right)}{k}\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)^{k}}.

Before proving Lemma
16
, we need to show a few more properties. Assuming the upper bound (
126
), (
131
) can be bounded above like

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k\right)}{k}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1}+s_{1}>0\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}+s_{n}>0\\
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{1}}}.

(141)

Simplifying the innermost sum in (
141
) is equivalent with a combinatorics problem: counting the number of
r_{1},\dots,r_{n},s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
r_{1}+s_{1},\dots,r_{n}+s_{n}>0
and
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}=k
. Analogously, this is equivalent with counting the number of ways that we can fit
k
indistinguishable balls into
n
ordered red and
n
ordered blue bins such that at least one ball ends up in the first red or blue bin, at least one ball ends up in the second red or blue bin, and so on. In turn, this is equal to the number of ways we can fit
n
out of
k
total indistinguishable balls into
n
ordered red and
n
ordered blue bins such that at least one ball ends up in the first red or blue bin, at least one ball ends up in the second red or blue bin, and so on, multiplied by the number of ways we can fit the remaining balls into the bins. The first of these quantities is
2^{n}
, as for each pair of red and blue bins, we can place one ball into either of the two bins, and there are a total of
n
pairs of red and blue bins. Hence, the second of these is the number of ways to put
\left(k-n\right)
unlabeled balls into
2n
distinct bins—i.e.,
[
30
]

\displaystyle\binom{k-n+2n-1}{2n-1}=\binom{k+n-1}{2n-1}=\frac{\left(k+n-1\right)!}{\left(k-n\right)!\left(2n-1\right)!},

(142)

but we can further rewrite (
142
) as the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\left(k+n-1\right)\left(k+n-2\right)\cdots\left(k-n+1\right)\left(k-n\right)!}{\left(k-n\right)!\left(2n-1\right)!}=\frac{nk^{2}\left(k^{2}-1^{2}\right)\cdots\left(k^{2}-\left(n-1\right)^{2}\right)}{kn^{2}\left(n^{2}-1^{2}\right)\cdot\left(n^{2}-\left(n-1\right)^{2}\right)}.

(143)

Thus, (
141
) can be simplified to

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k\right)}{k^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{2^{n}\frac{\left(k^{2}-0^{2}\right)\left(k^{2}-1^{2}\right)\cdots\left(k^{2}-\left(n-1\right)^{2}\right)}{\left(n^{2}-0^{2}\right)\left(n^{2}-1^{2}\right)\cdot\left(n^{2}-\left(n-1\right)^{2}\right)}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k\right)}{k^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{2\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}}.

(144)

As far as the authors know, the inner sum and product in (
144
) cannot be expressed in closed form.
However, we can use Riemann sums and properties of the LogSumExp function
[
18
]
to produce an upper bound which is saturated as
k\rightarrow\infty
as follows:

Lemma 17
.

For every
r>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}}{\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r\left(4+r\right)}}{2}\right)^{k}}}=1,

(145)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)}=\log\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r\left(4+r\right)}}{2}\right).

(146)

Furthermore, we have the following upper bound for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\leq k\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r\left(4+r\right)}}{2}\right)^{k}.

(147)

Proof.

The equivalence between (
145
) and (
146
) and the implication from

\displaystyle\max\left\{\int_{0}^{\max\left\{0,\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor-1\right\}/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}}{r}\right),\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.\int_{0}^{\left(\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil-1\right)/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}}{r}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\left\{\int_{0}^{\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy},\int_{0}^{\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}\right\}+\frac{\log k}{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r\left(4+r\right)}}{2}\right)+\frac{\log k}{k},

(148)

where
x_{+}=\sqrt{\frac{r}{4+r}}
, to (
147
) follow from sequential continuity and arithmetic properties of the logarithm
[
68
]
. Hence, it suffices to prove (
146
) and (
148
); at first, we show the latter. For any
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the following lower and upper bounds
[
18
]
:

\displaystyle\max\left\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\right\}\leq\log\left(\exp\left(x_{1}\right)+\dots+\exp\left(x_{k}\right)\right)\leq\max\left\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\right\}+\log k,

(149)

In particular, if we let
x_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}
for
n=1,\dots,k
and divide both sides by
1/k
, then (
149
) implies that

\displaystyle\max_{n=1,\dots,k}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{n=1,\dots,k}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}+\frac{\log k}{k}.

(150)

The reader might recognize the sum on the leftmost or rightmost sides of (
150
) as a Riemann sum for the following improper integral:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy},

(151)

where
x\coloneqq n/k
. Thus, we work along this vein of thought. At first, the integrand of (
151
) is increasing in
y
for
0\leq y\leq x\leq 1
. To show this, note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)=\frac{2y\left(1-x^{2}\right)}{\left(1-y^{2}\right)\left(x^{2}-y^{2}\right)},

(152)

and whenever
0<x<1
,
0<y<1
, and
\left|y\right|<\left|x\right|
, (
152
) is positive. Thus, for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
0\leq m\leq n-1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{m/k}^{\left(m+1\right)/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)dy}\leq\int_{m/k}^{\left(m+1\right)/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\int_{m/k}^{\left(m+1\right)/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}}=\int_{0}^{x}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}.

(153)

Furthermore, if we instead have that
1\leq m\leq n
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\left(m-1\right)/k}^{m/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)dy}\geq\int_{\left(m-1\right)/k}^{m/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}{\int_{\left(m-1\right)/k}^{m/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{n}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}}=\int_{0}^{x-1/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{x-1/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{x^{2}}{r}\right).

(154)

Since (
153
) and (
154
) hold for all
n=1,\dots,k
(or equivalently, for all
x=1/k,\dots,1
), (
150
), (
153
), and (
154
) imply that

\max_{x=1/k,\dots,1}\left\{\int_{0}^{x-1/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{x^{2}}{r}\right)\right\}\leq\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)\\
\leq\max_{x=1/k,\dots,1}\left\{\int_{0}^{x}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}\right\}+\frac{\log k}{k}.

(155)

We will see that the leftmost and rightmost sides of (
155
) are close for
k
large; there is no known closed-form expression for the leftmost side. We can maximize the rightmost side and then use the location of the maximum to approximate the leftmost side—i.e., for all
z=1/k,\dots,1
,

\int_{0}^{z-1/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{z^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{z^{2}}{r}\right)\\
\leq\max_{x=1/k,\dots,1}\left\{\int_{0}^{x-1/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{x^{2}}{r}\right)\right\}

(156)

Thus, to further simplify (
155
), we should compute
\int_{0}^{x}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}
, maximize the resultant expression over
all

x\in\left[0,1\right]
, then see how this implies the maximum over
x=1/k,\dots,1
. The derivative of
f(x)=\int_{0}^{x}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}
(with respect to
x
) is
f^{\prime}(x)=\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-x^{2}\right)}{4x^{2}}\right)
which 1) has two zeroes at
x_{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{r}{4+r}}
and 2) second derivative
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{2}{x^{3}-x}
. Hence, since
f^{\prime\prime}(x)<0
for
0<x<1
and
f^{\prime\prime}(x)>0
for
-1<x<0
, it is clear that
f
is maximized at
x_{+}=\sqrt{\frac{r}{4+r}}<1
on the domain
\left[0,1\right]
. Moreover, since
f
is always concave down on
\left[0,1\right]
, the maximum on the “grid”
\left\{1/k,\dots,1\right\}
will be either at
\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}
or
\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}
, since
x_{+}\in\left[\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k},\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right]
—i.e., one of the two multiples of
1/k
adjacent to
x_{+}
. Thus, (
155
) and (
156
) imply that

\displaystyle\max\left\{\int_{0}^{\max\left\{0,\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor-1\right\}/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}}{r}\right),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\int_{0}^{\left(\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil-1\right)/k}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}}{r}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\int_{0}^{\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy},\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\int_{0}^{\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}\right\}+\frac{\log k}{k},

(157)

which establishes part of (
148
). Furthermore, since
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}}=x_{+}
, taking limits of (
157
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x_{+}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x_{+}^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}-\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{x_{+}^{2}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{x_{+}}{\log\left(\frac{r\left(1-y^{2}\right)}{x_{+}^{2}-y^{2}}\right)dy}+\frac{\log k}{k}

(158)

for sufficiently large
k
. With help from Wolfram Mathematica
[
89
]
, the integral in (
158
) evaluates to the following:

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r(4+r)}}{2}\right).

(159)

Finally, since
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\frac{x_{+}^{2}}{r}\right)}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{+\frac{\log k}{k}}=0
, by the squeeze theorem
[
68
]
, (
158
) and (
159
) imply (
146
).

It remains to show the last inequality in (
148
). Note that
\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}
and
\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}
are “close” to
x_{+}
, but
f\left(\frac{\left\lceil{kx_{+}}\right\rceil}{k}\right)
and
f\left(\max\left\{\frac{1}{k},\frac{\left\lfloor{kx_{+}}\right\rfloor}{k}\right\}\right)
can still only be
at most

f\left(x_{+}\right)
. Hence, (
157
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\log\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{r\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}\right)\leq\log\left(\frac{2+r+\sqrt{r(4+r)}}{2}\right)+\frac{\log k}{k},

as we wanted to show. ∎

D.1.1
Proof of Lemma
16

Lemma
17
gives us a simple way to analyze the convergence and boundedness of the inner sum and product within (
144
). Hence, we have the proof of Lemma
16
:

Proof of Lemma
16
.

The equation (
145
) from Lemma
17
implies by the limit comparison test
[
68
]
that the convergence of (
144
) is equivalent with the convergence of

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\left(\eta\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)\right)^{k}B\left(k\right)}{k^{2}}}.

Moreover, (
147
) from Lemma
17
can be used to estimate (
144
) as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k\right)}{k^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}{\frac{2\left(k^{2}-m^{2}\right)}{n^{2}-m^{2}}}}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\eta^{k-1}B\left(k\right)}{k}\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)^{k}},

thereby proving Lemma
16
.
∎

D.2
Applications of the convergence criteria

We now explore some basic examples of functions
F
and
G
and see if the criteria above confirm an absolutely convergent modified Hamiltonian, expressed with Dynkin series in those cases. That being said, we invite readers to look for other functions
F
and
G
which satisfy (or fail to satisfy) the convergence criteria outlined in Theorems
8
,
9
, and
10
, or more generally, those from Lemmas
16
and
15
.

D.2.1
Quadratic case

In Section
8
and Appendix
C
, we computed the closed-form of the modified Hamiltonian, expressed with the Dynkin series when
f
and
g
are both quadratic functions. The convergence criteria we proposed in Appendix
D
are also satisfied.

Proposition 2
.

When
F=ap^{2},G=dq^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}

(160)

Thus, all convergence criteria in Appendix
D
are satisfied.

Proof.

We show (
160
) by induction. First, for the base case, it is trivial that

\displaystyle F=ap^{2}\leq\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{1},G=dq^{2}\leq\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{1}.

We now suppose that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|\leq\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}.

We may apply
F
or
G
to the IPBs. We start with the case of
F
.

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}+1}\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\},F\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}\cdot 4a\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}+1}\right|.

Similarly, for the case of
G
, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}G\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\},G\right\}\left(p,q\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}}\cdot 4d\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\max\{p^{2},q^{2},pq\}\left(\max\{4a,4d\}\right)^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}+1}\right|.

Hence, using (
160
) in tandem with any of the lemmas or theorems from Appendix
D
, the modified Hamiltonian for quadratic case converges absolutely for sufficiently small, nonzero
\eta
.

For a discussion about the conserved quantity predicted by
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
in this case, see Section
8
or Appendix
C
.
∎

D.2.2
Quartic case

We now present a case in which our lemma does not hold:
F=p^{4},G=q^{4}
.

Proposition 3
.

When
r_{1}=s_{1}=\cdots=r_{n}=s_{n}=1
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|=(n+1)(2n)!4^{2n-1}p^{2n+1}q^{2n+1}.

(161)

Thus, none of the convergence criteria in Appendix
D
can be satisfied in this case.

Proof.

We show this by induction. The formula is true for
n=1
, for
|\{p^{4},q^{4}\}|=16p^{3}q^{3}=2\cdot 2\cdot 4\cdot p^{3}\cdot p^{3}
. Suppose that the formula is true for
n
. We calculate the
n+1
-case:

\displaystyle|\{\{(n+1)(2n)!4^{2n-1}p^{2n+1}q^{2n+1},G\},F\}|

\displaystyle=|(n+1)(2n)!(2n+1)(2n+4)4^{2n+1}p^{2n+3}q^{2n+3}|

\displaystyle=|(n+2)(2n)!(2n+1)(2n+2)4^{2n+1}p^{2n+3}q^{2n+3}|

\displaystyle=\left((n+1)+1\right)2(n+1)!4^{2(n+1)-1}p^{2(n+1)+1}q^{2(n+1)+1}.

Hence, by induction, we have shown (
161
). But since even just the terms with IPBs that have
r_{1}=s_{1}=\cdots=r_{n}=s_{n}=1
grow with
(2n)!=\left(r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}+s_{1}+\dots+s_{n}\right)!
, none of the convergence criteria in Appendix
D
can be satisfied.
∎

It is not known whether the modified Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
actually converges in this case, although Figure
3
suggests that it
could
converge for sufficiently small
\eta>0
(or at least that a conserved quantity exists in that case).

D.2.3
Logarithmic case

To further demonstrate the limitations of the current convergence criteria derived in this paper, we calculate the case where
F=\log(a+p),G=\log(b+q)
.

Proposition 4
.

When
F=\log(a+p),G=\log(b+q)
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}}\right\}\right|=\frac{(S-2)!}{(a+p)^{S-1}(b+q)^{S-1}},\text{ where $S=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}$.}

Yet again, we claim that none of the convergence criteria in Appendix
D
are satisfied.

Proof.

As in the quadratic and quartic cases, we show this by induction. Note that
|\{F,G\}|=\frac{1}{(a+p)(b+q)}
, and hence the case where
s_{1}=r_{1}=1
is verified. Assume now that the formula is true for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, in which case

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G^{r_{1}}F^{s_{1}}G^{r_{2}}F^{s_{2}}\cdots G^{r_{n}}F^{s_{n}+1}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{(S-1)!}{(a+p)^{S}(b+q)^{S}}.

The case in which we apply
G
to the formula is very similar. However, the Fubini numbers
a_{k-1}\approx(k-1)!/\left(2\left(\log 2\right)^{k}\right)

[
75
]
, and hence, a factorial increase of the IPBs with
k
is too fast for the sums in either Lemma
15
or Lemma
16
to converge.
∎

However, there is still a known,
absolutely
convergent modified Hamiltonian for sufficiently small
\eta>0
. As shown in
[
31
]
and restated in Appendix
E
, the MH is of the form
F\left(\left(\alpha+p\right)\left(\beta+q\right)\right)
for an original Hamiltonian of the form
H\left(p,q\right)=\log\left(\alpha+p\right)+\log\left(\beta+q\right)
. This shows that these convergence criteria we derived, despite requiring sophisticated and careful techniques to derive, are sufficient but still
not
necessary to ensure that the MH converges.

Appendix E
List of known modified Hamiltonians

In this section, we review known choices of
F
and
G
such that when the symplectic Euler integrator (
5
) is applied to
F+G
, the iterations will conserve a modified Hamiltonian exactly. We remark that the results discussed here might not be exhaustive—these are only the cases that the authors are aware of in the literature. Here, we summarize the original Hamiltonian system and the modified Hamiltonian conserved by symplectic Euler (
5
) when applied to each respective system.

The quadratic case.

The quadratic case where
(p,q)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
H(p,q)=p^{\top}Bp+q^{\top}Cq
and
BC
is diagonalizable is well-studied in this paper. Refer to Theorem
3
or Appendix
C
for details.

The logarithmic case.

In
[
31
]
, the authors study the log case where
p,q\in\mathbb{R}
and

H(p,q)=\log\left(\alpha+p\right)+\log\left(\beta+q\right).

In this case, when
p,q
evolve under the symplectic Euler method (
5
), one can check the quantity
L(p,q)\coloneqq\log\left(\alpha+p\right)+\log\left(\beta+q\right)
is exactly conserved.

By using an action-angle argument,
[
31
]
derive the following closed form of the modified Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\eta}(p,q)=\log\left(1-\frac{\eta}{L(p,q)}\right)-\frac{\eta}{L(p,q)}\log\left(1-\frac{\eta}{L(p,q)}\right).

The lattice KdV equation case.

In
[
4
]
, the authors study the symplectic Euler integration of the following Hamiltonian system governed by lattice KdV equations:
p,q\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon
is a constant in
\mathbb{R}
,

H(p,q)=\epsilon\log\left(\epsilon^{2}-p^{2}\right)+\epsilon\log\left(\epsilon^{2}-q^{2}\right).

In this case, when
p,q
evolve under the symplectic Euler method (
5
), the quantity

\mathcal{I}=p^{2}q^{2}-\epsilon^{2}(p^{2}+q^{2})-2\epsilon\eta pq

is exactly conserved. Note that
\eta
in the above equation corresponds to the stepsize in the symplectic Euler method (
5
). By using an action-angle argument,
[
4
]
derive the following closed form of the modified Hamiltonian:

\tilde{H}_{\eta}(\mathcal{P})=\int^{\mathcal{P}}\int_{0}^{\frac{2\epsilon^{2}\eta\sqrt{-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}{\epsilon^{4}+\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\eta^{2}\epsilon^{2}q^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{2}-q^{2}\right)\left(\epsilon^{2}q^{2}+\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)}}dqd\mathcal{P}^{\prime},

where they use
\mathcal{P}
to denote
p^{2}q^{2}-\epsilon^{2}p^{2}-\epsilon^{2}q^{2}
. Note that the outer integral is an indefinite integral and the inner integral is a definite integral starting from
0
.

The Suris maps.

[
73
]
summarizes all possible Hamiltonian systems in
\mathbb{R}^{1\times 1}
with
F(p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2}
such that there exists a conserved quantity to the iterations of symplectic Euler (
5
).
[
73
]
also assumes that the conserved quantity,
\hat{H}(p,q)
, must be of the form
\hat{H}_{0}(p,q)+\eta\hat{H}_{1}(p,q)
, where both
\hat{H}_{0}(p,q)
and
\hat{H}_{1}(p,q)
are not functions of
\eta
.

Note that the existence of a conserved quantity is a necessary condition for the convergence of the Dynkin series (
20
) for
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
, since
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is itself a conserved quantity by Theorem
2
. To show this, suppose that
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}
is convergent yet there does not exist a conserved quantity for the iterations of symplectic Euler (
5
). Then, we arrive at a contradiction by Theorem 2.

Under these assumptions, a conserved quantity
\hat{H}
exists if and only if
G(q)
in the decomposition
H(p,q)=F(p)+G(q)
satisfies the following conditions, and takes the listed form. In the expressions listed below,
A,B,C,D,E
are arbitrary constants in
\mathbb{R}
.

\displaystyle F(p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2},\quad G(q,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\int_{x}\frac{A+Bx+Cx^{2}+Dx^{3}}{\eta-\eta^{2}(E+\frac{C}{3}x+\frac{D}{2}x^{2})}dx,

\displaystyle\hat{H}(p,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(p-q)^{2}-\eta\left[-\frac{1}{2}A(p+q)-\frac{1}{2}Bpq-\frac{1}{6}Cpq(p+q)-\frac{1}{4}Dp^{2}q^{2}-\frac{1}{2}E(p-q)^{2}\right];

\displaystyle F(p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2},\quad G(q,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\int_{x}\frac{2}{\omega\eta^{2}}\arctan\left(\frac{(\omega\eta/2)(A\sin\omega x+B\cos\omega x+C\sin 2\omega x+D\cos 2\omega x)}{1-(\omega\eta/2)(A\cos\omega x-B\sin\omega x+C\cos 2\omega x-D\sin 2\omega x+E)}\right)dx,

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\cos\omega(p-q)}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{\eta}{2\omega}\left[A(\cos\omega p+\cos\omega q)-B(\sin\omega p+\sin\omega q)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+C\cos\omega(p+q)-D\sin\omega(p+q)+E\cos\omega(p-q)\right];

\displaystyle F(p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2},\quad G(q,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\int_{x}\frac{1}{\alpha\eta^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{1+\alpha\eta(B\exp(-\alpha x)+D\exp(-2\alpha x)-E)}{1-\alpha\eta(A\exp(\alpha x)+C\exp(2\alpha x)+E)}\right)dx,

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh\alpha(p-q)-1}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{\eta}{2\alpha}\Big[-A(\exp(\alpha p)+\exp(\alpha q))+B(\exp(-\alpha p)+\exp(-\alpha q))

\displaystyle-C\exp(\alpha(p+q))+D\exp(-\alpha(p+q))-2E\cosh\alpha(p-q)\Big].

Appendix F
Partial proof of Conjecture
1

We start by taking the Taylor series of the
N
th modification
H_{N}
:

\displaystyle H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})=H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\Big((p_{n+1}-p_{n})\cdot\nabla_{p}+(q_{n+1}-q_{n})\cdot\nabla_{q}\Big)^{i}H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n}),

(162)

where the gradients are to be applied to
H_{N}
. Equivalently, by using the binomial theorem to expand (
162
), we have

H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})=\\
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{i}\binom{i}{j}\nabla_{p}^{j}\nabla_{q}^{i-j}H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})\left[\left(p_{n+1}-p_{n}\right)^{\otimes j},\left(q_{n+1}-q_{n}\right)^{\otimes\left(i-j\right)}\right]\right\},

(163)

where it is implied that the dimensions corresponding to
\nabla_{p}^{j}
multiply with those corresponding to
p_{n+1}-p_{n}
, and those for
\nabla_{q}^{i-j}
multiply with those for
q_{n+1}-q_{n}
.
7
7

7

This is suggested by the matching indices in
j
and
i-j
and the respectively similar ordering of
\nabla_{p}^{j}\nabla_{q}^{i-j}
with
\left[\left(p_{n+1}-p_{n}\right)^{\otimes j},\left(q_{n+1}-q_{n}\right)^{\otimes\left(i-j\right)}\right]
.

We proceed by plugging the iterates (
5
) of symplectic Euler into (
162
):

\displaystyle H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})=

(164)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{i}}{i!}\Big(-\nabla_{q}G\Big|_{q=q_{n}}\cdot\nabla_{p}+\nabla_{p}F\Big|_{p=p_{n+1}}\cdot\nabla_{q}\Big)^{i}H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n}),

(165)

or alternatively,

H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})=\\
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{i}}{i!}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{i}\left(-1\right)^{j}\binom{i}{j}\left[\nabla_{p}^{j}\nabla_{q}^{i-j}H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n}),\nabla_{q}G(q_{n})^{\otimes j},\nabla_{p}F(p_{n+1})^{\otimes\left(i-j\right)}\right]\right\}.

(166)

The fact that
\nabla_{p}F
is evaluated at
p_{n+1}
requires us to also approximate
F(p_{n+1})
with Taylor series:

\displaystyle F(p_{n+1})

\displaystyle=F\left(p_{n}-\eta\nabla_{q}G(q_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\left.F\left(p_{n}\right)-\eta\left.\left[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{q}G\right]\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2!}\left.\left[\nabla_{p}^{2}F,\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}-\frac{\eta^{3}}{3!}\left[\nabla_{p}^{3}F,\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}\right]\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\cdots

\displaystyle=F\left(p_{n}\right)+\eta\left.\{F,G\}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2!}\left.\{\{F,G\},G\}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\frac{\eta^{3}}{3!}\left.\{\{\{F,G\},G\},G\}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\cdots

\displaystyle=\left.e^{\eta\{\cdot,G\}}F\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}.

(167)

We proceed by plugging (
167
) into (
165
):

\displaystyle H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})=\left.\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{i}}{i!}\left[-\nabla_{q}G\left(q_{n}\right)\cdot\nabla_{p}+\left.\nabla_{p}\left(e^{\eta\{\cdot,G\}}F\right)\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}\cdot\nabla_{q}\right]^{i}H_{N}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}.

(168)

For (
168
), we note that the evaluations at
p_{n},q_{n}
both inside and outside the brackets are intentional. The reader should interpret this notation in the sense that, when the differential operator inside the brackets is repeatedly applied to
H_{N}
, the derivatives with respect to
p
and
q
do
not
apply to
\nabla_{q}G\left(q_{n}\right)
or
\left.\nabla_{p}\left(e^{\eta\{\cdot,G\}}F\right)\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}
, as one would normally expect from the product rule.

We proceed by rewriting the terms contained in
\widetilde{H}_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-\widetilde{H}_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})
as the sum of
N+1
Taylor series. Using the exponential operator series expansion, we can further rewrite (
168
) in the following form:

\displaystyle H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})=

\displaystyle\left.\left\{\exp\left[\eta\left(-\nabla_{q}G\left(q_{n}\right)\cdot\nabla_{p}+\left.\nabla_{p}\left(\exp\left(\eta\left\{\cdot,G\right\}\right)F\right)\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}\cdot\nabla_{q}\right)\right]-1\right\}H_{N}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}.

(169)

Then, by taking a Taylor series of (
169
) about
\eta=0
, the
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta^{j}\right)
term in (
169
) is

\displaystyle C_{j,k}\coloneqq\Omega_{j}(H_{k})\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{N},k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(170)

where
\Omega_{j}:C^{r+j}\left(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}\right)\rightarrow C^{r}\left(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}\right)
(
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) is the differential operator

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}\left(f\right)\coloneqq\frac{1}{j!}\left.\frac{\partial^{(j)}}{\partial\eta^{(j)}}\left\{\exp\left[\eta\left(-\nabla_{q}G\left(q_{n}\right)\cdot\nabla_{p}+\left.\nabla_{p}\left(\exp\left(\eta\left\{\cdot,G\right\}\right)F\right)\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}\cdot\nabla_{q}\right)\right]-1\right\}\right|_{\eta=0}f

(171)

for all
f\in C^{r+j}\left(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{R}\right)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

To get the conservation error of the
k
th-order truncated modified Hamiltonian,
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(k)}
, we combine the first
k
modifications and truncate the Taylor series at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta^{k+1}\right)
using the Lagrange form of the remainder from Taylor’s theorem. Hence, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 18
.

Suppose that the domains
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are convex and closed, and assume that
F
and
G
are sufficiently smooth. If the conditions

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\left.C_{m-j,j}\right|_{p,q}=0,\quad\text{ for all }~1\leq m\leq N+1,

(172)

and
|C_{m-j+1,j}|\leq L
hold for all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{Z}
, then we have that
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n},q_{n})\leq\eta^{N+2}(N+1)L
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are convex and closed,
\left(p_{n},q_{n}\right)
remains within
\mathcal{Z}
for all
n
, and we can apply the single-variable Taylor’s theorem with Lagrange remainder
[
68
, Theorem 31.3]
to expand (
169
) in the stepsize
\eta
about
\eta=0
, for any fixed truncation order
j+k=N
. Doing so gives

\displaystyle\left(H_{0}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{0}(p_{n},q_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\eta C_{1,0}+\eta^{2}C_{2,0}+\dots+\eta^{N+1}C_{N+1,0}\right]_{z=z_{n}}+\eta^{N+2}\left.C_{N+2,0}\right|_{z=\zeta_{0}}

\displaystyle\eta^{1}\left(H_{1}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{1}(p_{n},q_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\eta^{2}C_{1,1}+\eta^{3}C_{2,1}+\dots+\eta^{N+1}C_{N,1}\right]_{z=z_{n}}+\eta^{N+2}\left.C_{N+1,1}\right|_{z=\zeta_{1}}

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\left(H_{2}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{2}(p_{n},q_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\eta^{3}C_{1,2}+\eta^{4}C_{2,2}+\dots+\eta^{N+1}C_{N-1,2}\right]_{z=z_{n}}+\eta^{N+2}\left.C_{N,2}\right|_{z=\zeta_{2}}

\displaystyle\vdots\qquad

\displaystyle=\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\eta^{N}\left(H_{N}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-H_{N}(p_{n},q_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\eta^{N+1}\left.C_{1,N}\right|_{z=z_{n}}+\eta^{N+2}\left.C_{2,N}\right|_{z=\zeta_{N}}

(173)

for the order-
N
truncated modified Hamiltonian, where
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{N}
lie on the convex line segment
L_{n}\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:z=\left(1-\lambda\right)z_{n+1}+\lambda z_{n},0\leq\lambda\leq 1\right\}
between
z_{n}
and
z_{n+1}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n},q_{n})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{m=1}^{N+1}\eta^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\left.C_{m-j,j}\right|_{p_{n},q_{n}}+\eta^{N+2}\sum_{m=0}^{N}C_{N+2-m,m}|_{z=\zeta_{m}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{m=1}^{N+1}0+\eta^{N+2}\sum_{m=0}^{N}C_{N+2-m,m}|_{z=\zeta_{m}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{N+2}\sum_{m=0}^{N}\left|C_{N+2-m,m}|_{z=\zeta_{m}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{N+2}(N+1)L.

(174)

∎

It now remains to show that the sum along each diagonal in (
173
) actually cancels to zero except for that which corresponds to the last coefficient at order
\eta^{N+2}
. To partially answer this question, in the next two subsections, we show that the following lemma holds:

Lemma 19
.

For
N=0,1,2,3
, if
F:\mathcal{P}\times\mathbb{R}
and
G:\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{R}
have derivatives up to order
N+2
on their respective domains, then
we have that
\sum_{j=0}^{i}C_{i+1-j,j}=0
for all
i\leq N
on all of
\mathcal{Z}
. Moreover, for
N=0,1,\dots,10
, when
d=1
and
F,G
have derivatives up to order
N+2
on their respective domains, then we have

\sum_{j=0}^{i}C_{i+1-j,j}=0
for all
i\leq N
on
\mathcal{Z}
.

Once Lemma
19
has been proven, we could use that and Lemma
18
to show that
\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n+1},q_{n+1})-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{(N)}(p_{n},q_{n})=\mathcal{O}(\eta^{N+2})
. However, at least up to
j+k=5
(i.e., up to
N=3
), we will show that each
C_{j,k}
can be expressed as a linear combination of products of higher-order derivatives of
F
and
G
such that, for each product in this linear combination, the sum of the orders of derivatives in the product adds up to
2\left(j+k\right)=2N+4
, no individual derivative is of higher order than
j+k=N+2
, and there are
N+3
of such terms. Thus, we can show Conjecture
1
for (in theory) arbitrarily high
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by assuming that
F
and
G
are both
L
-smooth of orders
1,\dots,N+2
, since in that case, (
174
) can be simplified to

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(N\right)}\left(p_{n+1},q_{n+1}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(N\right)}\left(p_{n},q_{n}\right)\right|\leq\Phi\left(N\right)L^{N+3}\eta^{N+2}

(175)

for some function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
to be determined.

Using the aforementioned steps, we show how to verify (
175
) for orders
N\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and for arbitrary
d
in Appendix
F.1
by direct computation.
We also show how this can be verified by computer for orders
N\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}
and
d=1
in Appendix
F.2
.

F.1
Verification up to
N=3
via direct computation

We start by computing
\Omega_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,5
, since all will be used when checking Conjecture
1
up to
N=3
.

Calculation of
\Omega_{j}
up to
j=5

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{p}(\cdot)\nabla_{q}G+\nabla_{q}(\cdot)\nabla_{p}F

(176)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q}(\cdot)\nabla_{p}\{F,G\}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}(\cdot)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]-\nabla_{pq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}(\cdot)\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]

(177)

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{q}(\cdot)\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]+\nabla_{pq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]-\nabla_{qq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{ppp}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}(G)^{\otimes 3}]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{ppq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{p}F]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}]+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{p}(F)^{\otimes 3}]

(178)

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{pppp}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 4}]-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{pppq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3},\nabla_{p}F]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{ppq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{ppqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]]

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla_{pqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{pqqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{q}(\cdot)\nabla_{pppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{ppp}{F}[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}(\cdot)[(\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G)^{\otimes 2}]+\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{qqqq}(\cdot)[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 4}]

(179)

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{120}\nabla_{ppppp}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 5}\right]+\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{ppppq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 4},\nabla_{p}F\right]+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{pppq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3},\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{pppqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3},\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]+\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{ppq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{ppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{ppqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right]+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{ppqqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{pq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{pppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}\right]\right]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{ppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pqqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G,\left(\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right)^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{pqqqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 4}\right]+\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{q}\left(\cdot\right)\nabla_{ppppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 4}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}\right]\right]+\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{qqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{ppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3},\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{p}F,\left(\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G\right)^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{ppp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right]+\frac{1}{120}\nabla_{qqqqq}\left(\cdot\right)\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 5}\right].

(180)

For
N=0
:

Note
C_{1,0}
equals to
\Omega_{1}(H_{0})
. Hence,

\displaystyle C_{1,0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{1}(H_{0})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{p}(H_{0})+\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{q}(H_{0})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{p}F+\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{q}G

\displaystyle=0.

(181)

Thus, the case where
N=0
is verified. And in this case, we do not need to get an upper bound on the remainder, as that will be taken care of when dealing with when
N=1
.

For
N=1
:

We first calculate
\Omega_{2}(H_{0})
and
\Omega_{1}(H_{1})
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(H_{0})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q}(H_{0})\nabla_{p}\{F,G\}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}(H_{0})\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]-\nabla_{pq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}(H_{0})\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}G\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right],

(182)

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(H_{1})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{p}(H_{1})\nabla_{q}G+\nabla_{q}(H_{1})\nabla_{p}F=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}].

(183)

We now sum (
182
) and (
183
) up:

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(H_{0})+\Omega_{1}(H_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}G\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle=0.

(184)

Thus, the case where
N=1
is verified. Furthermore, to get an upper bound on the
N=0
case, assuming that
F
and
G
are
L
-smooth of orders
1
and
2
, (
174
) gives us

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(0\right)}\left(p_{n+1},q_{n+1}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(0\right)}\left(p_{n},q_{n}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{2}\max_{z\in L_{n}}\left|\Omega_{2}\left(H_{0}\right)\left(z\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\left(\max\left|\nabla_{pp}F\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]\right|+\max\left|\nabla_{qq}G\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq L^{3}\eta^{2}.

The second inequality above follows from triangle inequality, and the last from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

For
N=2
:

We first calculate
\Omega_{3}(H_{0})
,
\Omega_{2}(H_{1})
and
\Omega_{1}(H_{2})
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}(H_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{q}(H_{0})\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]+\nabla_{pq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla_{qq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{ppp}(H_{0})[\nabla_{q}(G)^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{ppq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2},\nabla_{p}F]-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pqq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{p}G,\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}]+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqq}(H_{0})[\nabla_{p}(F)^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}]-0-\nabla_{qq}G[\nabla_{p}F,\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G]-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}(G)^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad+0+0+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}(F)^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]-\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}(F)^{\otimes 3}],

(185)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(H_{1})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q}(H_{1})\nabla_{p}\{F,G\}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{pp}(H_{1})\left[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla_{pq}(H_{1})[\nabla_{q}G,\nabla_{p}F]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{qq}(H_{1})\left[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{p}F-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]+\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{p}F-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}],

(186)

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(H_{2})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{p}(H_{2})\nabla_{q}G+\nabla_{q}(H_{2})\nabla_{p}F

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}].

(187)

We now sum (
185
), (
186
) and (
187
):

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}(H_{0})+\Omega_{2}(H_{1})+\Omega_{1}(H_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]-\nabla_{p}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{q}G+\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}(F)^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]+\nabla_{q}G\nabla_{pp}F\nabla_{qq}G\nabla_{p}F-\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{ppp}F[\nabla_{q}G^{\otimes 3}]+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{qqq}G[\nabla_{p}F^{\otimes 3}]

\displaystyle=0

(188)

Thus, the case where
N=2
is verified. Moreover, to get an upper bound on the
N=1
case, assuming that
F
and
G
are
L
-smooth of orders
1
,
2
, and
3
, (
174
) gives us

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(1\right)}\left(p_{n+1},q_{n+1}\right)-\widetilde{H}_{\eta}^{\left(1\right)}\left(p_{n},q_{n}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{3}\left(\max_{z\in L_{n}}\left|\Omega_{3}\left(H_{0}\right)\left(z\right)\right|+\max_{z\in L_{n}}\left|\Omega_{2}\left(H_{1}\right)\left(z\right)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{3}L^{4}\left(\frac{1}{3}+1+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{4}+1+\frac{1}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3L^{4}\eta^{3}.

For
N=3
:

We first calculate
\Omega_{4}(H_{0})
,
\Omega_{3}(H_{1})
,
\Omega_{2}(H_{2})
and
\Omega_{1}(H_{3})
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}(H_{0})
